Fig. 1
Average displacement

Abstract
We consider the matrix composite materials (CM) of either random (statistically homogeneous or inhomogeneous), periodic, or deterministic (neither random nor periodic) structures. CMs exhibit linear or nonlinear behavior, coupled or uncoupled multi-physical phenomena, locally elastic, weakly nonlocal (strain gradient and stress gradient), or strongly nonlocal (strain-type and displacement-type, peridynamics) phase properties. A modified Computational Analytical Micromechanics (CAM) approach introduces an exact Additive General Integral Equation (AGIE) for CMs of any structure and phase properties. This method utilizes a unified iteration solution for static AGIEs, incorporating a new universal training parameter based on body forces with compact support. CAM based on a general integral equation (GIE) and proposed by the author before, is modified by the introduction of the exact AGIE which applies to CMs of any structure and phase properties mentioned above. The unified iteration solution of static AGIEs is adapted to the body force with compact support serving as a fundamentally new universal training parameter. The approach also establishes a critical threshold for filtering out unsuitable sub-datasets of effective parameters through a novel Representative Volume Element (RVE) concept, which extends Hill’s classical framework. This RVE concept eliminates sample size, boundary layer, and edge effects, making it applicable to CMs of any structure and phase properties, regardless of local or nonlocal, linear or nonlinear. Incorporating this new RVE concept into machine learning and neural network techniques enables the construction of any unpredefined surrogate nonlocal operators. The methodology is structured as a modular, block-based framework, allowing independent development and refinement of software components. This flexible, robust AGIE-CAM framework integrates data-driven, multi-scale, and multi-physics modeling, accelerating research in CM of any microtopology and phase properties considered.



Unified Micromechanics Theory of Composites

Valeriy A. Buryachenko

1
Introduction

Predicting the behavior of composite materials based on phase properties and microstructure is a fundamental challenge in micromechanics. Stress field estimations rely on solving a basic problem: an inclusion within an infinite homogeneous matrix under a uniform effective field. When constituents exhibit local elasticity, the solution for an ellipsoidal inclusion in a homogeneous field is given analytically by Eshelby’s tensor
[
165
]
(see also
[
372
]
,
[
511
]
).
For inclusions of arbitrary shapes, various numerical methods have been developed. Finite element analysis (FEA) and truncation methods approximate the infinite medium with an extended sample. Direct modeling of an infinite medium is achieved using boundary integral equation (BIE) methods
[
315
]
,
[
80
]
or volume integral equation (VIE) approaches
[
54
]
. Comprehensive reviews of micromechanical methods can be found in
[
54
]
,
[
80
]
,
[
143
]
,
[
247
]
,
[
355
]
, and
[
443
]
.

Conventional micromechanics assumes materials can be treated as a continuum at arbitrarily small scales, with properties and behaviors that are invariant to time and length scales, excluding size effects
[
232
]
. However, real material models need to define an “effective” set of properties, approximating microstructural details below a certain resolution
[
31
]
. In cases where scales (material and field) are comparable, nonlocal elasticity theory, developed by authors like Kröner, Eringen, Kunin, and Bažant, incorporates lattice theory with classical elasticity to address these intermediate effects
(see
[
161
]
,
[
164
]
,
[
273
]
,
[
266
]
,

[
275
]
,
[
276
]
,
[
277
]
,
as well as
[
6
]
,
[
31
]
,
[
32
]
,
[
104
]
,
[
163
]
,
[
289
]
,
[
337
]
,
[
447
]
see also comprehensive rewievs
[
406
]
,
[
444
]
,
[
446
]
).
“Generalized continuum mechanics,” as defined by
[
338
]
, covers models beyond the Cauchy framework.
Generalized continua can be broadly divided into weakly and strongly nonlocal theories.
Weakly nonlocal theories
involve higher-order models where material points have additional degrees of freedom beyond classical translation, such as rotation
[
117
]
, stretch
[
163
]
, or higher-order displacement derivatives
[
197
]
,
[
198
]
,
[
347
]
,
[
348
]
, which were essentially developed later (see, e.g., the comprehensive reviews
[
6
]
,
[
31
]
,
[
377
]
). The Cosserat theory
[
117
]
treats materials as rigid particles with independent rotations, while the micromorphic theory
[
160
]
,
[
162
]
models a material as particles that can move, deform, and rotate. Micromorphic theory generalizes Cosserat and micropolar theories, incorporating microstretch behavior.

Strongly nonlocal elasticity
theory explicitly models long-range interactions using convolutions of kinematical variables, which represent the material’s degrees of freedom. This approach contrasts with weakly nonlocal theories, where nonlocality is introduced implicitly through gradients of micro-deformations and coupling variables. In strongly nonlocal theories, stress is an integral functional of strain, with a kernel that weights material properties. Key contributions to this field came from Kröner
[
267
]
,
[
271
]
, and Kunin
[
276
]
in the 1960s generalized in

[
164
]
,
[
277
]
,
[
389
]
. These models can be divided into

strain-type
and
displacement-type
(see
[
164
]
,
[
267
]
,
[
271
]
,
[
275
]
,
[
276
]
,
[
279
]
,
[
389
]
), with the displacement-type model reducible to the strain-type model
[
278
]
.

Under certain conditions, strain gradient elasticity can approximate integral non-local theory. Nonlocal microcontinuum mechanics
[
163
]
,
[
164
]
was developed as a more general form of nonlocal mechanics. Eringen
[
164
]
presented a unified approach to nonlocal field theories for various media: elastic solids, viscous
fluids, electromagnetic solids, and fluids, memory-dependent elastic
solids, and media with microstructure.
Peridynamics (PD)
, introduced by Silling
[
414
]
and discussed in
[
338
]
and
[
424
]
, is a special displacement-type nonlocal theory that eliminates displacement derivatives.
Peridynamics by Silling
[
414
]
(see also
[
42
]
,
[
137
]
,
[
241
]
,
[
329
]
,
[
367
]
,
[
415
]
,
[
423
]
,
[
424
]
), revolutionized solid mechanics by replacing local partial differential equations with integral equations that avoid spatial derivatives of displacement. In this approach, the equilibrium at a material point is determined by the sum of internal forces from surrounding points within a defined ”horizon.” Unlike classical mechanics, which relies on local interactions, peridynamics uses a
state-based model
(SB) where the deformation at a point is influenced by all bonds within the horizon. This method naturally handles discontinuities like cracks, making it ideal for studying failure and damage propagation.
The
bond-based
(BB) peridynamic approach models interactions between pairs of points within a horizon but limits the Poisson’s ratio in isotropic materials to specific values of
\nu=1/4
(3D and 2D plane strain) or
\nu=1/3
(2D plane stress)
[
414
]
. The correspondence model
(see see
[
3
]
,
[
420
]
)
allows peridynamics to replicate the classical elasticity tensor for fully anisotropic materials. Non-ordinary state-based models offer more flexibility but can be unstable, requiring careful numerical methods for accurate results (see
[
449
]
for details). In contrast, the ordinary state-based model
[
396
]
overcomes these issues by accurately reproducing any component of the classical continuum mechanics (CCM) elasticity tensor in both 2D and 3D. Tensor-based peridynamics
[
441
]
expands on the BB model, enhancing its adaptability for both isotropic and anisotropic materials.

Foundational principles for thermoperidynamic media under uniform volumetric boundary loading were proposed using the linearized bond-based peridynamic approach (see
[
61
]
,
[
68
]
,
[
85
]
). A key outcome is that the effective behavior of these media can be described by a local effective constitutive equation, similar to classical thermoelasticity

[
54
]
. The relationship between effective properties and influence functions, which describe mechanical interactions within the material, highlights how one region’s behavior affects others. This is achieved by decomposing local fields into load and residual fields, allowing an energetic definition of the effective elastic moduli. There is a notable similarity between locally elastic composites and peridynamic composites, as both follow principles like Hill’s condition and the self-adjoint nature of the peridynamic operator. This allows classical solutions to be extended to nonlocal peridynamic systems, offering a contrast to simplified methods like mixture theory and scale separation hypothesis (see
[
17
]
,
[
18
]
,

[
19
]
,
[
113
]
,
[
134
]
,
[
179
]
,
[
225
]
,

[
227
]
,
[
297
]
,
[
329
]
,
[
331
]
,
[
332
]
,

[
340
]
,
[
386
]
,

[
467
]
,
[
468
]
,
[
473
]
,
[
481
]
).

The expansion of Local Micromechanics (LM) from classical continuum mechanics (CCM) to nonlocal phenomena (see
[
61
]
,
[
68
]
,
[
80
]
,
[
94
]
) is driven by the General Integral Equation (GIE, see
[
61
]
,
[
68
]
,
[
80
]
) for microinhomogeneous media. This method, also called computational analytical micromechanics (CAM), does not rely on Green’s functions or specific constitutive laws, making it more versatile. The GIE significantly improves accuracy in local field estimations and can even lead to correcting the sign of estimations inside inclusions (see
[
80
]
)

In locally elastic composite materials (CMs) under inhomogeneous loading, effective deformations are governed by nonlocal operators (differential or integral, see
[
54
]
,
[
80
]
,
[
145
]
,
[
417
]
,
[
453
]
), which relate the statistical average of fields at a point to those in the surrounding area. These operators improve material modeling accuracy, especially for inhomogeneous media, nonlinear or nonlocal laws, and coupled processes. The CAM is particularly effective for addressing multiscale and multiphysics problems, enabling precise simulations of small-scale effects by incorporating homogenized surrogate models. This framework provides a robust approach for analyzing random and periodic structure CMs.

The study of periodic structure composites benefits from the regularity of their microstructure, allowing for specialized homogenization techniques
[
175
]
,
[
191
]
,
[
515
]
. Asymptotic homogenization, introduced by Babuska, analyzes composites where the unit cell is much smaller than the overall material size, approximating the effect of the unit cell on the global response (e.g.,
[
24
]
,
[
175
]
). In contrast, computational homogenization explicitly resolves the material’s microscopic behavior to determine macroscopic quantities like stress and deformation, making it effective for modeling complex behaviors such as nonlinear or inelastic properties (e.g.,
[
263
]
,
[
335
]
,
[
440
]
).

Madenci and colleagues
[
325
]
(see also
[
135
]
,
[
136
]
,
[
228
]
,
[
306
]
,
[
326
]
, and
[
454
]
) advanced computational homogenization by introducing the peridynamic unit cell model, which extends traditional techniques to the peridynamic framework using classical periodic boundary conditions (PBC). The development of volumetric periodic boundary conditions (VPBC) in
[
71
]
,
[
73
]
,
[
80
]
further generalized these methods for peridynamics. A key innovation in peridynamic homogenization is determining effective material moduli by averaging tractions and displacements along the unit cell boundary
[
71
]
,
[
73
]
,
[
80
]
, offering a simpler, more efficient alternative to methods requiring volume averages of stresses and strains inside the unit cell

[
183
]
,
[
186
]
, and
[
228
]
. The latter methods are more complex and less general because they require differentiating displacement fields, which may lack smoothness.

The representative volume element (RVE) is crucial for accurately predicting the macroscopic properties of heterogeneous materials. The correct RVE size ensures proper representation of microstructural heterogeneity while avoiding boundary or size effects. Hill’s classical definition
[
217
]
requires macroscopically homogeneous boundary conditions and defines the effective behavior with a tensor of moduli. RVE size determination involves balancing the capture of microstructural heterogeneities with scale separation, achieved when
a\ll\Lambda\ll L
(see Subsection 2.3 for details). The smallest size at which properties stabilize is the appropriate RVE. The concept of statistically equivalent representative volume element (SERVE) uses image-based modeling to generate accurate computational domains, with further details found in
[
27
]
,
[
252
]
,
[
335
]
,
[
353
]
,
[
366
]
, and others.
When the scale separation hypothesis is violated, statistically homogeneous fields no longer apply, leading to nonlocal coupling between stress and strain averages, mediated by a tensorial kernel. This requires using an effective elastic operator in integral form. Nonlocal operators include strongly nonlocal methods (strain type and displacement type, peridynamics) and weakly nonlocal models like strain- or stress-gradient methods. Micromechanics helps bridge coupled scales governed by nonlocal constitutive equations. Instead of classical effective moduli
[
217
]
, effective nonlocal operators, often in integral or differential form, are required, prompting a redefinition of the representative volume element (RVE). This applies to both random (
[
139
]
,
[
140
]
,
[
141
]
) and periodic (
[
14
]
,
[
264
]
,
[
265
]
,
[
426
]
) composites. The RVE concept is crucial for analyzing nonlocal effects from inhomogeneous fields, material nonlocality, and inclusion interactions.

Machine learning (ML) and neural network (NN) techniques have significantly advanced nonlocal operator theory, improving generality and flexibility. Early work by Silling
[
418
]
and
[
487
]
(later expanded upon in
[
489
]
) used Direct Numerical Simulations (DNS) for the construction of surrogate integral operators. Recent developments include nonlocal neural operators that map function spaces
[
286
]
,
[
307
]
, and various NN architectures like DeepONet, PCA-Net, and Fourier Neural Operators (FNO)
[
195
]
,
[
229
]
. Peridynamic Neural Operator (PNO) was proposed in
[
239
]
and subsequently extended to the
the Heterogeneous Peridynamic Neural Operator (HeteroPNO) in
[
240
]
.
Additionally, Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) embed physical laws directly into the neural network as constraints
(see
[
118
]
,
[
206
]
,
[
210
]
,
[
222
]
,
[
253
]
,
[
260
]
,
[
383
]
, and
[
388
]
).
Combining neural operators with PINNs enables modeling complex systems with nonlinearities, heterogeneity, and nonlocality
[
171
]
,
[
195
]
,
[
455
]
.

However, despite the power of ML and NN techniques, they often neglect crucial micromechanical factors like size scale, edge effects, and RVE, important for both linear and nonlinear materials. To overcome this, the proposed CAM generates new compressed datasets for complex material structures (whether random, periodic, or deterministic), using a novel RVE concept that doesn’t depend on constitutive laws or surrogate operator forms. Instead, it focuses on field concentration factors within material phases. These datasets, incorporating the new RVE concept, must be used with any ML or NN methods predicting nonlocal surrogate operators, ensuring accurate predictions by eliminating size scale, boundary, and edge effect issues.

The structure of the paper is as follows: Section 2 offers a concise overview of peridynamic theory, tailored to support the subsequent analysis. It includes a discussion on the statistical representation of composite microstructures and describes the volumetric homogeneous displacement loading conditions, alongside certain field averages. Section 3 introduces the decomposition of both material and field parameters. General Integral Equations (GIEs) and Additive GIEs are considered, incorporating either statistical average fields or fields induced in an infinite matrix by a body force with compact support., respectively.
Iterative solutions of AGIE are presented for either random or periodic structure CMs in Sections 4 and 5, respectively. Estimation of field fluctuations and effective energy-based criteria are presented in Section 6. AGIEs and datasets are considered in Section 7 for CMs with locally elastic, weakly nonlocal (strain gradient and stress gradient), and strongly nonlocal (strain-type) properties.
Classical RVE concepts generalized to nonlocally elastic media are considered in Section 8. A novel RVE concept is introduced specifically for cases involving a body force with compact support. By applying translation averaging to the GIE solutions for periodic structure CMs, statistical averages of fields are derived. Section 9 leverages the body force with compact support as a training parameter to develop a suite of surrogate effective operators of any
predefined form for CMs with either random, periodic, or deterministic structures and a wide class of phase properties.

2
Preliminaries.

2.1
Basic equations of peridynamics

One considers a linear elastic medium occupying an open simply connected bounded domain
w\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}

with a smooth boundary
\Gamma_{0}
and with an indicator function
W
and space dimensionality

d
(
d=1,2,3
).
The domain
w
with the boundary
\Gamma^{0}
consists from a homogeneous matrix
v^{(0)}
and a statistically homogeneous
field
X=(v_{i})
of heterogeneity
v_{i}
with indicator functions,
V_{i}
and bounded by the closed
smooth surfaces
\Gamma_{i}

(i=1,2,\ldots)
.
It is presumed that the heterogeneities can be grouped into phases
v^{(q)}\quad(q=1,2,\ldots,N)
with identical mechanical and geometrical properties.
The basic equations of local thermoelasticity of composites are considered

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\ \ {\rm or}\ \ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf M$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\nabla{\otimes}{\bf u}+(\nabla{\otimes}{\bf u})^{\top}]/2,\ \ \nabla\times\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\times\nabla={\bf 0},

(2.3)

where
\otimes
and
and
\times
are the tensor and vector products, respectively, and
(.)^{\top}
denotes a matrix transposition.
It is presumed that the body force density (forcing term)
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
is self-equilibrated
and vanished outside some loading region
B^{b}
:

\int\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})={\bf 0},\ \ \ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}\not\in b({\bf 0},B^{b}):=\{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}|\ |\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}|\leq B^{b}\},

(2.4)

where
b(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},B^{b})
is the ball of radius
B^{b}

centered at
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i}={\bf 0}
.

{\bf L(x)}
and
{\bf M(x)\equiv L(x)}^{-1}
are the known phase
stiffness and compliance tensors.

\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
are second-order tensors of local eigenstrains and eigenstresses.
In particular, for isotropic
phases, the local stiffness tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
is presented in
terms of the local bulk
k(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
and shear
\mu(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

moduli and:

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=(dk,2\mu)\equiv dk(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\mbox{\boldmath$\bf N$}_{1}+2\mu(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\mbox{\boldmath$\bf N$}_{2},\ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\beta^{t}\theta\mbox{\boldmath$\delta$},

{\bf N}_{1}=\mbox{\boldmath$\delta$}\otimes\mbox{\boldmath$\delta$}/d,\ {\bf N}_{2}={\bf I-N}_{1}

(d=2\ {\rm or}\ 3
) whereas

\delta
and
\bf I
are the unit second-order and fourth-order tensors;
\theta=T-T_{0}
denotes the temperature changes with respect to a reference temperature
T_{0}
and
\beta^{t}
is a thermal expansion.
For all material tensors
\bf g
(e.g.,
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf M$},\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$},\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$})
the notation
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf g$}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\equiv\mbox{\boldmath$\bf g$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf g$}^{(0)}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf g$}^{(m)}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v^{(m)},\ m=0,1
) is exploited.
The upper index
(m)
indicates the
components, and the lower index
i
shows the individual
heterogeneities;
v^{(0)}=w\backslash v
,
v\equiv\cup v_{i},\ V(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\sum V_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
, and
V_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
are the
indicator functions of
v_{i}
, equals 1 at

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}
and 0 otherwise,
(i=1,2,\ldots)
.
Substitution of Eqs. (
2.2
) and (
2.4
) into Eq. (
2.1
) leads to a representation of the equilibrium equation (
2.1
) in the form

^{L}\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})={\bf 0},\ \ \ ^{L}\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}):=\nabla[\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})],

(2.5)

where
{}^{L}\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
is an elliptic differential operator of the second order.

In this section, a summary of the linear peridynamic model introduced
by Silling
[
414
]
(see also ,
[
295
]
,
[
329
]
,
[
419
]
,
[
423
]
,
[
424
]
) is presented.
An equilibrium equation is free of any derivatives of displacement (contrary to Eq. (
2.5
)) and presented in the following form

\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})={\bf 0},\ \ \ \widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}):=\int\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})d\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}},

(2.6)

where
\bf f
is a
bond force density
whose value is the force vector
that the point located at
\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}
(in the reference configuration) exerts on the point located at
\bf x
(also in the reference configuration); the third argument
\bf x
of

\bf f
(
2.6
) can be dropped for the homogeneous media.
Equations (
2.5
1
) and (
2.6
1
) have the same form for both local and peridynamic formulation with
the different operators (
2.5
2
) and (
2.6
2
). Because of this, the superscripts
{}^{L}(\cdot)

will correspond to the local case.

The relative position of two material points in the reference configuration
\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}=\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}
and their relative displacement
\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
are connected with the relative position vector between the two points in the deformed
(or current) configuration
\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}
.
Only points
\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}
inside some neighborhood (horizon region)
{\cal H}_{\bf x}
of
\bf x
interact with
\bf x
:

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\equiv{\bf 0}\ \ \ \forall\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}\not\in{\cal H}_{\bf x}.

(2.7)

The vector
\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}=\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}
(
\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}\in{\cal H}_{\bf x}
) is called a
bond
to
\bf x
, and the collection of all bonds to
\bf x
form the horizon region
{\cal H}_{\bf x}
.
Without a loss of generality, it is assumed that a shape of
{\cal H}_{\bf x}
is spherical:
{\cal H}_{\bf x}=\{\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}:\ |\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|\leq l_{\delta}\}
and a number
l_{\delta}
, called the
horizon
, does not depend on
\bf x
. The properties of

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
are concidered in
[
414
]
.

Peridynamic states introduced by Silling
[
420
]
(for a more detailed summary, see
[
424
]
)) are the functions acting on bounds.
There are scalar, vector, and modulus states producing the scalars, vectors, and
2nd order tensors, respectively.
\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf T$}}[\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}]\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\mbox{$\rangle$}
and
\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf T$}}[\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}]\mbox{$\langle$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\mbox{$\rangle$}
are the
force vector states
at
\bf x
and

\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}
, which return the force densities associated with
\xi
and
-\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}
, respectively.
In the ordinary state-based peridynamics being considered,
\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf T$}}[\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}]\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\mbox{$\rangle$}
is parallel (in contrast with the non-ordinary model) to the deformation vector state and the equilibrium Eq. (
2.6
) is expressed through the
force vector states as (see for details
[
420
]
,
[
424
]
)

\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})={\bf 0},\ \ \ \widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}):=\int\{\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf T$}}[\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}]\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\mbox{$\rangle$}-\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf T$}}[\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}]\mbox{$\langle$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\mbox{$\rangle$}\}d\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}.

(2.8)

A small displacement state
\bf u
when

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathfrak{l}$}:=\sup_{|\bf\xi|\leq{\it l}_{\delta}}|\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$})\mbox{$\rangle$}|\ll l_{\delta}.

(2.9)

is considered.
Force vector state

\displaystyle\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf T$}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf T$}}^{0}+\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{K}$}}\bullet\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}.

(2.10)

is expressed through the
modulus state

\mathbb{K}
.
Here, the operation of
dot product

\bullet
of two vector states
\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf A$}}
,
\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf B$}}
and a double state

\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}}
are introduced as

\displaystyle\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf A$}}\bullet\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf B$}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\langle\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf A$}}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\mbox{$\rangle$}\cdot\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf B$}}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\mbox{$\rangle$}\big\rangle^{\cal H_{\bf x}}=\int_{\cal H_{\bf x}}\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf A$}}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\mbox{$\rangle$}\cdot\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf B$}}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\mbox{$\rangle$}~d\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},

\displaystyle(\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}}\bullet\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf B$}})_{i}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\mbox{$\rangle$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\cal H_{\bf x}}\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}}_{ij}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}\mbox{$\rangle$}\cdot\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf B$}}_{j}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}\mbox{$\rangle$}~d\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}.

(2.11)

Hereafter
\mbox{$\langle$}(\cdot)\mbox{$\rangle$}^{\cal H_{\bf x}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
denotes the average over the horizon region
{\cal H_{\bf x}}
with the center
\bf x
.

A linearized model for pure mechanical loading (
\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}\equiv{\bf 0}
) can be written from (
2.8
2
) as described by Silling
[
415
]

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bf 0},

(2.12)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}):

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{w}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$})-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}))~dV_{q},

(2.13)

where the integrand in Eq. (
2.10
) vanishing at

|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$}|\geq 2l_{\delta}
may be non-null at
l_{\delta}<|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$}|<2l_{\delta}
.
The kernel with the following symmetries holds for any
\bf x
and
\bf q
:

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{\top}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}).

(2.14)

Thermoelastic cases (
\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}\not\equiv{\bf 0}
) were considered in

[
329
]
,
[
34
]
,
[
258
]
,
[
330
]
.
Fully coupled thermo-mechanical PD theory was proposed in
[
369
]
,
[
478
]
.

For subsequent convenience, one introduces a vector-valued function
\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf p$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$})=\begin{cases}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf p$})-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$}),\mbox{\boldmath$\bf p$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$}),&{\rm if}\ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf p$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$}\in w,\\
{\bf 0},&{\rm otherwise},\end{cases}

(2.15)

which is presumed to be Riemann-integrable.
Then, one can
define the “peridynamic stress”
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})
at the point
\bf z

as the total force that all material points
\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}
to the right of
\bf z

exert on all material points to its left
(see e.g.,
[
80
]
,
[
295
]
,
[
425
]
,
[
458
]
).
For
dD
cases (
d=1,2,3
)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}),

(2.16)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{S}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}(y+z)^{d-1}\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}+y\mbox{\boldmath$\bf m$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-z\mbox{\boldmath$\bf m$})\otimes\mbox{\boldmath$\bf m$}dzdyd\Omega_{\bf m}.

(2.17)

Here,
S
stands for the unit sphere, and
d\Omega_{\bf m}
denotes a differential solid angle on
S
in the direction of any unite vector
\bf m
. It was proved
[
299
]
that the peridynamic stress is the same as the
first Piola-Kirchhoff static
Virial stress which offers a simple and clear expression for numerical calculations of
peridynamic stress.

The equilibrium Eqs. (
2.8
), (
2.21
) and (
2.16
) of ordinary state-based PD are considered.
When the interactions between material points are only pairwise, the equilibrium equations are reduced to the bond-based PD equations. One of the simplest nonlinear is
the proportional microelastic material model
[
419
]

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}^{\rm bond}(\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!f(|\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}|,\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$})\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$},\ \ \ f(|\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}|,\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$})=c(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$})s,

(2.18)

\bf e

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\!\!\frac{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}}{|\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}|},\ \ \ s:=\frac{|\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}|-|\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}|}{|\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}|},

(2.19)

where
s
denotes the bond stretch (also called bond strain) which is the relative change of the length of a bond, and
c
is referenced as the “bond constant”. Although this model is linear in terms of the bond stretches, it is nonlinear in terms of displacements;
thermoelastic cases (
\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}\not\equiv{\bf 0}
) were considered in

[
34
]
,
[
258
]
,
[
329
]
,
[
330
]
. A nonlinear model in terms of
bond stretches (at
\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}\equiv{\bf 0}
)

f(|\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}+\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}|,\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$})=c(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$})[s+3s^{2}/2+s^{3}/2]

(2.20)

is considered in
[
237
]
. The potential role of employing a nonlinear peridynamic kernel
in predicting the onset of fractures has been explored in
[
129
]
(see also
[
115
]
,

[
116
]
).

A linearized version of the theory (for small displacement) for a microelastic homogeneous
material (
2.21
) takes the form (
\forall\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$})

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}^{\rm bond}(\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}_{\rm lin}^{\rm bond}(\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{\rm bond}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$},

(2.21)

Here, the material’s
micromodulus
function
\bf C

contains all constitutive information and its
value is a second-order tensor given by

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{\rm bond}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\frac{\partial\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}({\bf 0},\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})}{\partial\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}\ \ \ \forall\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}.

(2.22)

Substitution of Eq. (
2.21
) into Eq. (
2.6
) leads to the equilibrium equation

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{\rm bond},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})={\bf 0},

(2.23)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{\rm bond},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{\rm bond}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$})-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}))~dV_{q}.

(2.24)

For consistency with Newton’s third law,
the micromodulus function
\bf C
for the homogeneous materials must be symmetric
to its tensor structure as well as to arguments

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{\rm bond}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{\rm bond}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{\rm bond})^{\top}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$})\ \ \ \forall\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$},

(2.25)

where the properties of
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{\rm bond}
are discussed in detail in Silling [54]
(see also [10, 33]). For example, for the micromodulus functions with the step-function and triangular profiles,

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}V({\cal H}_{\bf x}),\ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}(1-|\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}|/l_{\delta})V({\cal H}_{\bf x}),

(2.26)

respectively, where
V({\cal H}_{\bf x})
is the indicator function of
{\cal H}_{\bf x}
.
The peridynamic solution
of Eq. (
2.23
) with
\bf C
described by Eq. (
2.26
) is investigated in detail by both
numerical and analytical methods in 1D (see
[
44
]
,
[
343
]
,
[
425
]
,
[
458
]
),
2D (see
[
225
]
,
[
226
]
), and 3D cases
[
344
]
,
[
459
]
.

For bond-based peridynamics, the stress representation (
2.17
) can be recast in terms of displacements

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}):

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{S}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}(y+z)^{d-1}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{\rm bond}((y+z)\mbox{\boldmath$\bf m$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-z\mbox{\boldmath$\bf m$})

(2.27)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle[\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}+y\mbox{\boldmath$\bf m$})-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-z\mbox{\boldmath$\bf m$})]\otimes\mbox{\boldmath$\bf m$}dzdyd\Omega_{\bf m}.

It is interesting that equilibrium Eqs. (
2.22
) and (
2.23
) formally coincide, although the kernels

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$})
(
2.23
) and
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{\rm bond}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$})
(
2.21
) are conceptually different with different symmetry properties (
2.15
) and (
2.25
), respectively. Moreover, in the state-based version, the maximum interaction distance between the points is
2l_{\delta}
whereas this distance in bond-based peridynamics coincides with the horizon
l_{\delta}
.
However, this similarity provides a possibility to reformulate the results obtained before for the linear bond-based peridynamic micromechanics (see for details
[
80
]
and
[
94
]
) to their counterparts for the linear ordinary state-based ones.

2.2
Volumetric boundary conditions

Owing to nonlocality, the equilibrium equation (
2.6
)
is combined with a “boundary” condition, used as a volumetric constraint in the so-called interaction domain
w_{\Gamma}
(in opposite to the local case where the boundary conditions are imposed directly at the bounding surface
\Gamma^{(0)}
, see for details
[
414
]
,
[
257
]
; i.e., the nonlocal boundary
w_{\Gamma}
is a
d
-dimensional region
unlike its
(d-1)
-dimensional counterpart
\Gamma^{0}
in local problems.
The interaction domain
w_{\Gamma}

contains points
\bf y
not in
w
interacting with points
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in w
.
The most popular shape for
w_{\Gamma}
with prescribed either the forces or displacements is a boundary layer
of thickness given by
2l_{c}

(see
[
323
]
):

w_{\Gamma}=\{w\oplus{\cal H}_{\bf 0}\}\backslash w
, where
w\oplus{\cal H}_{\bf 0}
is the Minkowski sum
w
(
{\cal A}\oplus{\cal B}:=\cup_{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in{\cal A},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}\in{\cal B}}\{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}+\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}\}
); then
w
is the internal region of

\overline{\overline{w}}
(see
[
414
]
).

It is presumed that
\overline{\overline{w}}
is considered as a cutting out of a macrodomain (containing a statistically large number of inclusion’s realization) from the random
heterogeneous medium covering the entire space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then, the Dirichlet, or Neumann volumetric boundary conditions (VBC, see
[
146
]
),
[
341
]
)
are called homogeneous volumetric loading conditions if there exist some symmetric constant tensors either

\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}^{w_{\Gamma}}
or
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}^{w_{\Gamma}}
such that

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf h$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}^{w_{\Gamma}}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$},\ \forall\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}\in w_{\Gamma u}=w_{\Gamma},

(2.28)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf g$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})=-\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}^{w_{\Gamma}}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf n$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}),\ \forall\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}\in w_{\Gamma\sigma}=w_{\Gamma},

(2.29)

respectively.
There are no specific restrictions on the smoothness and shape of
\Gamma_{0}
, which is defined only by the convenience of representation.
It should be mentioned that in the LM, the analogs of the VBC (
2.28
) and (
2.29
) (at the nonlocality vanishing
l_{\delta}\to 0
) are the homogeneous boundary conditions (also called the
kinematic uniform boundary conditions (KUBC) and static uniform boundary
conditions (SUBC), respectively)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}^{w_{\Gamma}}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$},\ \forall\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}\in\Gamma_{0u}={\Gamma}_{0},

(2.30)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf t$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}^{w_{\Gamma}}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf n$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}),\ \forall\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}\in\Gamma_{0\sigma}={\Gamma}_{0},

(2.31)

correspond
to the analyses of the equations for either strain or stresses, respectively, which are formally similar to each other. However,
in peridynamic micromechanics, a primary unknown variable is displacement (rather than stresses), and, because of this, the VBC
(
2.28
) is assumed.

Seemingly to the volumetric boundary domain
w_{\Gamma i}
, we introduce a volumetric interface boundary (called also interaction interface, see
[
400
]
,
[
11
]
)

v_{\Gamma i}=v_{\Gamma i}^{+}\cup v_{\Gamma i}^{-}
where

v_{\Gamma i}^{+}
and
v_{\Gamma i}^{-}
are the boundary layers (internal and external, respectively) divided by the geometric boundary
\Gamma_{i}
and have a thickness expressed through the horizon as
2l_{\delta}

The geometrical boundaries of the boundary layers

v_{\Gamma i}^{+}
and
v_{\Gamma i}^{-}
are

\Gamma_{i}^{+}
and
\Gamma_{i}^{-}
, respectively.
For a general form of the inclusion
v_{i}
the external volumetric interface
\Gamma^{-}_{i}
can be expressed through the Minkowski sum

\Gamma^{-}_{i}=\{v_{i}\oplus 2{\cal H}_{\bf 0}\}\backslash v_{i}
, where

2{\cal H}_{\bf 0}:=\{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|\ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}/2\in{\cal H}_{0}\}
.
A nonlocal closure of the inclusion
v^{l}_{i}:=v_{i}\oplus 2{\cal H}_{\bf 0}
(with an indicator function
V_{i}^{l}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
) is called an
extended inclusion
while
v^{l}:=\cup v_{i}^{l}
(
i=1,2,\ldots
) (with the indicator function
V^{l}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\sum_{i}V_{i}^{l}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
) stands for the extended inclusion phase. In so doing
v^{l(0)}:=w\backslash v^{l}\subset v^{(0)}
is called a
truncated matrix
.

In the simplest case, a micromodulus of interaction interface of the inclusion
v_{i}
(see, e.g.
[
11
]
,
[
12
]
,

[
419
]
) is presented as an average value of the material properties at two material points connecting dissimilar materials
(
V^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+V_{i}(\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})=V^{(0)}(\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})+V_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=1
)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})=[\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})+\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})]/2

(2.32)

although more sophisticated models of interaction interface properties (see, e.g.,
[
325
]
,

[
287
]
, see for references
[
40
]
), can be incorporated into
the general subsequent representations. In particular, a variation of
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})
was presented in
[
325
]

and
[
380
]

in a spirit of the functionally graded materials theory described in, e.g.
[
54
]
.

2.3
Descriptions of random and periodic structures of the composite microstructures

We consider a popular group of composites, called matrix composites, which consists of a continuous matrix phase reinforced by isolated inhomogeneities of various shapes.
Three material length scales (see, e.g.,
[
443
]
,
[
498
]
) are considered:
the macroscopic scale
L
, characterizing the extent of
w
, the microscopic scale
a
, related with the
heterogeneities
v_{i}
, and the horizon
l_{\delta}
. Moreover, one supposes that the applied field varies on a characteristic length scale
\Lambda
.
The limit of our interests for both the material
scales and field one are either

\displaystyle L\geq\Lambda\geq a^{\rm int}\geq a\geq l_{\delta}\ \ \

\displaystyle{\rm or}

\displaystyle\ \ \ L\gg\Lambda\gg a^{\rm int}\geq a\gg l_{\delta},

(2.33)

\displaystyle L\geq\Lambda\geq|\Omega_{00}|\geq l_{\delta}\ \ \

\displaystyle{\rm or}

\displaystyle\ \ \ L\gg\Lambda\gg|\Omega_{00}|\gg l_{\delta},

(2.34)

where the inequalities (
2.33
2
) and (
2.34
2
) are called a scale separation hypotheses.
The inequalities (
2.33
) correspond to the case of random structure CMs, where
a^{\rm int}
stands for the scale of long-range interactions of inclusions (e.g.
a^{\rm int}=6a
). The inequalities (
2.34
) describe the scale representations for periodic structure CMs, where
\Omega_{00}
is a unite cell (see for details Subsection 2.4), and, for shortening, we use
|\Omega_{00}|
instead of linear
|\Omega_{00}|^{1/d}
.

The random quantities of the statistically homogeneous random fields (see, e.g.
[
80
]
,
[
333
]
)
are described by a conditional
probability density
\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i}|v_{1},{\bf x}_{1}

for finding a heterogeneity of type
i
with the center
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i}
in the domain
v_{i}
, with the fixed heterogeneities
v_{1}
centered at
{\bf x}_{1}
.
The notation
\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i}|;v_{1},{\bf x}_{1})
denotes the case
{\bf x}_{i}\neq{\bf x}_{1}
.
We have
\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i}|;v_{1},{\bf x}_{1})=0
(since heterogeneities cannot overlap) for values of
{\bf x}_{i}
placed inside the
some domain
v^{0}_{1}
called “excluded volumes”, where
v^{0}_{1}\supset v_{1}

with indicator function
V^{0}_{1}
is the “excluded volumes” of
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i}
with respect to
v_{1}
,
and
\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i}|;v_{1},{\bf x}_{1})\to\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})

as
|{\bf x}_{i}-{\bf x}_{m}|\to\infty
,
m=1,\ldots,n
(since no long-range order is assumed).

\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x})
is a number density,
n^{(k)}=n^{(k)}({\bf x})
of component
v^{(k)}\ni v_{i}

at the point
{\bf x}
and
c^{(k)}=c^{(k)}({\bf x})
is the concentration, i.e. volume fraction,
of the component
v_{i}\in v^{(k)}
at the point
{\bf x}
:

c^{(k)}({\bf x})=\langle V^{(k)}\rangle({\bf x})=\overline{v}_{i}n^{(k)}({\bf x}),\ \overline{v}_{i}={\rm mes}v_{i}\ \ (k=1,2,\ldots,N;\ i=1,2,\ldots),\quad c^{(0)}({\bf x})=1-\langle V\rangle({\bf x}).

Additionally to the average
\langle(.)\rangle({\bf x})
, the notation

\langle(.)|v_{1},{\bf x}_{1}\rangle({\bf x})

will be used for the conditional average taken
for the ensemble of a statistically homogeneous
set
X=(v_{i})
at the point
{\bf x}
,
on the condition that there is heterogeneity at
the point
{\bf x}_{1}
and

{\bf x}_{i}\neq\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{1}
.
The notation
\langle(.)|;v_{1},{\bf x}_{1}\rangle({\bf x})

is exploited for the additional condition
{\bf x}\notin v_{1}
. In a general case
of
statistically inhomogeneous
media with the homogeneous matrix (e.g., for so
called F
Functionally Graded Materials
, FGM) the conditional probability density is not invariant with respect to
translation, that is, the microstructure functions depend on their absolute positions

[
382
]
:

\displaystyle\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i}|;v_{1},{\bf x}_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i}|;v_{1},{\bf x}_{1}+\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\ \ \ (\forall\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in R^{d})

\displaystyle\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i}|;v_{1},{\bf x}_{1})

\displaystyle\not=

\displaystyle\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i}|;v_{1},{\bf x}_{1}+\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\ \ \ (\exists\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in R^{d}).

(2.35)

for statistically homogeneous and inhomogeneous media, respectively.

Of course, any deterministic (e.g. periodic) field of inclusions
v
with the centers
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{\alpha}\in\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}
can be presented by
the probability density
\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})
and conditional probability density

\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i}|;v_{j},{\bf x}_{j})
expressed through the
\delta
functions (
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{\bf\alpha}\in\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}
)

\displaystyle\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\bf\alpha}\delta(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{\bf\alpha}),

\displaystyle\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i}|;v_{j},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\bf\alpha}\delta(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{\bf\alpha})-\delta(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j}).

(2.36)

In more detail, a periodic structure of CM is considered.
For simplicity of notations for periodic media, we consider 2D cases

w=\cup\Omega_{ij}
(
i,j=0,\pm 1,\pm 2,\ldots
)
with the square
unit cells
\Omega_{ij}
and the centers of the unite cells
\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}=\{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{ij}\}
. Let a representative unit cell
\Omega_{00}

with the corner points
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}^{c}_{kl}
(
k,l=\pm 1
) has the boundary

\Gamma^{0}=\cup\Gamma^{0}_{ij}
where the boundary partition
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{ij}^{0}\in\Gamma^{0}_{ij}
separates the UCs
\Omega_{00}
and
\Omega_{ij}
(
i=0,\pm 1,\ j=\pm(1-|i|)
, see Fig. 19.2 in
[
80
]
).
A representative volume element (or the unit cell, UC)
\Omega_{00}
is deformed in a repetitive way as its neighbors.
The position vectors
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}^{0}_{ij}\in\Gamma^{0}_{ij}
or

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}^{0}_{kl}\in\Gamma^{0}_{kl}
are presented by the corner
points
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}^{c}_{mn}
(
i=0,\pm 1,\ j=\pm(1-|i|),\ k=-i,\ l=-j,\ m,n=\pm 1)
; e.g.,

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}^{0}_{ij}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}^{0}_{kl}+\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}^{c}_{1,-1}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}^{c}_{-1,-1}

for the
i=1
,
j=0
.

N
field points
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i}
(
i=1,\ldots,N
) in the central
cube
\Omega_{00}=\{[-l^{\Omega},l^{\Omega}]^{d}\}
are periodically reflected as

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i}^{\bf\alpha}
into the neighboring cubes
\Omega_{\bf\alpha}
, where

\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d})\in Z^{+}
, and
\alpha_{i}=0,\pm 1

(i=1,\ldots,d)
.

A volumetric unit cell boundary
{\Omega}_{\Gamma}=\Gamma^{+}\cup\Gamma^{-}
contains
the boundary layers
\Gamma^{+}
and
\Gamma^{-}
(internal and external, respectively) separating by the geometric boundary
\Gamma^{0}
and having a thickness given by the horizon
l_{\delta}
(the UC
\Omega_{00}
with some parts of the internal and external volumetric boundaries is presented in Fig. 1).
For a general form of the UC
\Omega_{00}
the external volumetric boundary
\Gamma^{-}
can be expressed through the Minkovski addition

\Gamma^{-}=\{\Omega_{00}\oplus{\cal H}_{\bf 0}\}\backslash\Omega_{00}

with the partitions
\Gamma_{\bf\alpha}^{-}=\Gamma^{-}\cap\Omega_{\alpha}

(if
|\alpha_{1}|+\ldots+|\alpha_{d}|=d-1
)
and
\Gamma_{\bf\alpha}^{c-}=\Gamma^{-}\cap\Omega_{\alpha}
(if
|\alpha_{1}|+\ldots+|\alpha_{d}|=d
); for
d=3
and
|\alpha_{1}|+|\alpha_{2}|+|\alpha_{3}|=1
,
the case
\Gamma^{-}\cap\Omega_{\alpha}
corresponds to the edges.
For
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in\Omega_{00}
and
{\cal H}_{\bf x}\subset\Omega_{00}
(i.e.
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\not\in\Gamma^{+}
), we don’t need to take the interaction of the opposite volumetric boundaries into account.
However, for
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in\Gamma^{+}
, some points of the family
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}\in{\cal H}_{\bf x}
don’t fall into the UC
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}\not\in\Omega_{00}
that required establishment of the tying constraints linking both the position points and the fields of the opposite volumetric boundaries
\Gamma^{+}
and
\Gamma^{-}
.
If the source point

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{p}+\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\in\Omega_{\bf\alpha}

(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\in{\mbox{$\cal H$}}_{p}
) then
the peridynamic counterpart of the local
periodic boundary conditions (PBC) called

volumetric periodic boundary conditions
(VPBC, see
[
72
]
,
[
80
]
) represent periodic displacements and antiperiodic tractions. So, the homogeneous VBC at the remote boundary (
2.28
) is equivalent to
the VPBC

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{p}+\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{p}+\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}-2\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{l})+2\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}^{w_{\Gamma}}\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{l},

(2.37)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf t$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}^{0}_{\bf\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf t$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}^{0}_{\bf\gamma}),

(2.38)

where
\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}=-\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}^{0}_{\bf\alpha}
are defined analogously
to
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}^{0}_{ij}
. The VPBCs (
2.37
) and (
2.38
) coincide with the classical PBC

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{p})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{p}-2\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{l})+2\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}^{w_{\Gamma}}\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}^{l},

(2.39)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf t$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}^{0}_{\bf\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf t$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}^{0}_{\bf\gamma})

(2.40)

only for
l_{\delta}=0
.
The VPBC (
2.37
) and (
2.38
) were proposed in
[
72
]
,
[
80
]
for any peridynamic constitutive lows of phases; it was applied to CMs
with both the bond-based peridynamic properties of constituents
[
72
]
,
[
80
]

and non-ordinary state-based
[
183
]
peridynamic properties of phases. For the general forcing term
(
2.4
) (instead of VBC (
2.28
)), analogs of VPBC (
2.37
) and (
2.38
) are not defined.

The VPBC (
2.37
) was proposed for the case that the inclusion
v_{i}
does not intersect the boundary
\Gamma^{0}
of the UC
\Omega^{00}
.
So, for
{\rm dist}(v_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\Gamma^{0})\geq l_{\delta}^{(0)}
(for
\forall\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}
and
\forall v_{i}\subset\Omega_{00}
) and
l_{\delta}^{(1)}\leq l_{\delta}^{(0)}
, the VPBC (
2.37
) holds.
However, if the inclusion
v_{i}
does not intersect the boundary
\Gamma^{0}
of the UC
\Omega^{00}

then the VPBC (
2.37
) should be corercted by replacement of
\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\in{\mbox{$\cal H$}}_{p}
by
\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\in{\mbox{$\cal H$}}^{\rm 0+1}_{p}
, where

{\mbox{$\cal H$}}^{\rm 0+1}_{p}
is a horizon region with the radius
l_{\delta}^{(0)}+l_{\delta}^{(1)}
.
Micromodulus
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})
corresponding to the points
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{p}
and
\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{p}+\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}

is given by the formula

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})=\begin{cases}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}),&{\rm for}\ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}\in v,\\
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}),&{\rm for}\ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}\in v^{(0)},\\
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}),&{\rm for}\ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v,\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}\in v^{(0)}{\rm or}\ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v^{(0)},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}\in v,\end{cases}

(2.41)

where the bonds connecting points in the different materials are characterized
by micromodulus
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{i}
, which can be chosen such that, e.g. (
2.37
).
Although there are no technical issues with the correction of
discretized peridynamic equation in the extended interface
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{\Gamma}
, it is helpful to assume
for simplicity only that the peridynamic operator in the extended interface (fuzzy interface, i.e.
not sharp as it would be in a local formulation) is
described by Eq. (
2.6
) with the constant horizon and micromodulus (
2.41
) determined as an average
value of the micromoduli in the matrix and inclusion (
2.32
).
The adaptive grid refinement technique was proposed in
[
43
]

for analysis of peridynamic problems in the vicinity of the interface that involves a variable horizon
size.

It should be mentioned that the most attractive tool of ML and NN techniques in PM of periodic structure CMs is using a general case of forcing term (
2.4
) as a training parameter (see Subsection 4.7). Then solution periodicity is lost and PBC (
2.39
) (and VPBC (
2.37
)) cannot be fulfilled. This problem of correction of PBC (
2.39
) even in LM is not solved for the general case of forcing term (
2.4
). However, this challenge is effortlessly surmounted in Subsection 8.3.

2.4
Some averages

In the case of statistically homogeneous random functions
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
, the ergodicity condition is assumed when the spatial average is estimated over
one sufficiently large sample and statistical mean coincide for both the whole volume
w
and the individual constituent
v^{(k)}
(
k=0,1,\ldots,N
):

\langle
\bf f
\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}\}\equiv\lim_{w\uparrow\mathbb{R}^{d}}|w|^{-1}\int_{w}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},

(2.42)

\displaystyle\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{(k)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}\}^{(k)}\equiv\lim_{w\uparrow\mathbb{R}^{d}}|w|^{-1}\int_{w}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})V^{(k)}d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},

(2.43)

were
|w|={\rm mes}\,w
.
Under the Gauss theorem, the volume averages by integrals over the corresponding boundary are expressed.
In particular, for the homogeneous boundary conditions either
(
2.28
) or (
2.29
)
the mean value
\{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\}
or
\{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\}
of
\varepsilon
or
\sigma

coincide with

\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}^{w_{\Gamma}}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}^{w_{\Gamma}}
(see, e.g.
[
80
]
).

The volume averages of the strains and stresses inside the extended inclusion
v^{l}_{i}

can be presented by the averages over the external inclusion boundary

\Gamma_{i}^{-}

by the use of Gauss’s theorem

\displaystyle\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}V^{l}\mbox{$\rangle$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}^{l(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}^{l\omega(1)}:=\frac{1}{\bar{w}}\sum_{i}\int_{\Gamma_{i}^{-}}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf s$}){\,}{\,}^{\,{}^{S}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\otimes\mbox{\boldmath$\bf n$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf s$})\,d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf s$},

(2.44)

\displaystyle\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}V^{l}\mbox{$\rangle$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}^{l(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}^{l\omega(1)}:=\frac{1}{\bar{w}}\sum_{i}\int_{\Gamma^{-}_{i}}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf t$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf s$}){\,}{\,}^{\,{}^{S}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\otimes\mbox{\boldmath$\bf s$}\,d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf s$}.

(2.45)

The absence of numerical differentiation defines an advantage of the surface average.
(
2.44
) with respect to the volume average.
The surface average of stresses
(
2.45
) has the well-known advantage of the
reduction of dimension by one that can be crucial for the analyst. In Eqs. (
2.44
) and (
2.45
) the averages over the modified phases (e.g., the extended inclusions or truncated matrix) are used

\displaystyle\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{l(k)}=\{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}\}^{l(k)}\equiv\lim_{w\uparrow\mathbb{R}^{d}}|w|^{-1}\int_{w}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})V^{l(k)}d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},

(2.46)

instead of the averages (
2.42
2
) exploited in local micromechanics.

It should be mentioned that all averages(
2.42
)-(
2.46
) and the equality

\{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf g$}\}=c^{(0)}\{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf g$}\}^{(0)}+c^{(1)}\{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf g$}\}^{(1)},

(2.47)

(e.g.,
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf g$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$},\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$},\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}
),
is fulfilled only for statistically homogeneous media subjected to the homogeneous boundary conditions.
For periodic structure CM, the CMl is constructed using the
building blocks or cells:

w=\cup\Omega_{\bf m}
containing the inclusions
v_{\bf m}\subset\Omega_{\bf m}
.
Hereafter the notation

{\bf f}^{\small\Omega}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

will be used for the average of the function
{\bf f}

over the cell
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in\Omega_{i}
with the center

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}^{\Omega}_{i}\in\Omega_{i}
:

{\bf f}^{\Omega}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})={\bf f}^{\Omega}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i}^{\Omega})\equiv n(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\int_{\Omega_{i}}{\bf f}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})~d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$},\quad\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in\Omega_{i},

(2.48)

n(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\equiv 1/\overline{\Omega}_{i}

is the number density of inclusions in the cell
\Omega_{i}
.

Let
\mbox{$\cal V$}_{\bf x}
be a “moving averaging” cell (or moving-window
[
80
]
,

[
196
]
) with the center
\bf x

and characteristic size
a_{{\cal V}}=({\overline{\mbox{$\cal V$}}})^{1/d}
, and let
for the sake of definiteness

\chi
be a random vector uniformly distributed on
\mbox{$\cal V$}_{\bf x}

whose value at
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}\in\mbox{$\cal V$}_{\bf x}
is

\varphi_{\small\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})=1/\overline{\mbox{$\cal V$}}_{\bf x}
and

\varphi_{\small\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})\equiv 0
otherwise. Then
we can define the average of the function
{\bf f}
with respect to
translations of the vector
\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$}:

\langle{\bf f}\rangle_{\bf x}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})={1\over\overline{\mbox{$\cal V$}}_{\bf x}}\int_{\mbox{$\cal V$}_{\rm\bf X}}{\bf f}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})~d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\quad\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in{\Omega}_{i}.

(2.49)

Among other things, “moving averaging” cell
\mbox{$\cal V$}_{\bf x}

can be obtained by translation of a cell
\Omega_{i}

and can vary in size and shape during motion from point
to point.
To make the exposition clear we will
assume that
\mbox{$\cal V$}_{\bf x}
results from
\Omega_{i}
by translation of the vector

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}^{\Omega}_{i}
; it can be seen, however, that this assumption is not
mandatory.

3
Decomposition of the material and field parameters

3.1
Decomposition of the material parameters

The operators
\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})
(
2.24
) (and
{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}^{\sigma}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$},\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$})
(
2.16
),
are linear ones with respect to the arguments, e.g.,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}_{1}+\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}_{2},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}_{1},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})+\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}_{2},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}),

\displaystyle\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}_{1}+\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}_{1})+\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}_{2}).

(3.1)

For the matrix CMs with a homogeneous matrix, we decompose the material parameters
as

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{(0)}+\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})+\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}),

(3.2)

where
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}):=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{(0)}
and

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}):=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})
are the jumps of material properties with respect to the of the matrix.
Obviously,
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\equiv{\bf 0}
vanishes outside the inclusion phase
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\not\in v
whereas
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})\equiv{\bf 0}
outside the extended inclusion phase

v^{l}:=v\oplus{\mbox{$\cal H$}}_{0}
(obtained by a Minkovski sum of

v
when either
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\not\in v^{{\rm}l}
or

\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}\not\in v^{{\rm}l}
).

In a similar manner, the peristatic operators
\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})
(
2.24
),
and the stress operator

\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}^{\sigma}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})
(
2.27
) can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})+\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}),

\displaystyle\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}^{\sigma}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}^{\sigma(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})+\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}_{1}^{\sigma}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}),

(3.3)

where
\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})
(
2.24
) and

\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}^{\sigma(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})
(
2.27
) are estimated at
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})
.
In so doing,
\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})
(
2.24
) and
\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}^{\sigma}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})
(
2.27
)
vanish inside the truncated matrix
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v^{(0)l}:=w\backslash v^{l}
.

Peridynamic counterpart of the tensorial decomposition for local elasticity

\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{(0)}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\ \ \ ^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}):=\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})-^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

(3.4)

can be presented in the next form for
the operator (
2.17
)

\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),

(3.5)

were
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma(0)}
denotes an action of the operator

\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma}
on the medium with the material properties of the matrix defined by the bond force

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}^{(0)}
[for example, for the bond force (
2.8
), we have
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})\equiv\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$})
]
and the displacement fields
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}({\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}})
of the real CM.
The jump operator
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
defined by Eq. (
3.5
)
is called the
local stress polarization tensor
[compare with Eq. (
3.5
)] and represented
as

\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})={1\over 2}\int_{S}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}(y+z)^{d-1}\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}+y\mbox{\boldmath$\bf m$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-z\mbox{\boldmath$\bf m$})\otimes\mbox{\boldmath$\bf m$}dzdyd\Omega_{\bf m},

(3.6)

where

\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf p$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$}):=\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf p$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$})-\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf p$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$}).

In particular, for the linear bond force (
2.8
), the bond forces
\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf p$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$})
and
\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}}_{0}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf p$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$})

called also the
micropolarization
tensors

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}}_{\rm 1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}(\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}(\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

(3.7)

are expressed through the material parameters (
3.1
).

3.2
Analytica and Computational Micromechanics in LM

The effective field hypothesis (EFH) dates back to Poisson, Faraday, Mossotti, Clausius, Lorenz, and Maxwell (1824-1879, see
[
80
]
)
who proposed EFH
H1a
as a local homogeneous
field acting on the inclusions (
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}
)

\displaystyle\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})={\rm const}

(3.8)

and differing from the applied macroscopic one (at infinity).
This concept of EFH (forming the first background of analytical micromechanics, see for details
[
54
]
) has directed the development of micromechanics (even if the term EFH was not used) over the last 150 years (daily and globally) and contributed to their progress incomparable with any other concept of analytical micromechanics.

Buryachenko
[
95
]
and
[
96
]
presented general classifications of Analytical Micromechanics (AMic) and Computational Micromechanics (CMic) methods.
So, the concept of the EFH (even if this term is not mentioned) in combination with subsequent assumptions
totally predominates (and creates the fundamental limitations) in all four groups of
Analytical Micromechanics
(AMic, classification by
Willis
[
466
]
) of
random
random structure matrix CMs in physics and mechanics of heterogeneous media:

\displaystyle{\rm\underline{Gr1)}\ model\ methods,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \underline{Gr2)}\ perturbation\ methods,}

\displaystyle{\rm\underline{Gr3)}\ variational\ methods,\ \ \ \underline{Gr4)}\ self-consistent\ methods},

(3.9)

see for references and details

[
54
]
,
[
80
]
,
[
143
]
,
[
247
]
,
[
443
]
.
The ultimate goal of AMic is to develop more cheap, fast, robust, and more flexible methods (for making
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{*}
estimations)
than direct numerical simulation (DNS), although it takes additional intellectual complexity to
the implementations.

In contrast,
Computational Micrmechanics
(CMic) for CM of
deterministic
structures is based on DNS which can be found by different numerical methods. Computational micromechanics can be classified into three broad categories (blocks):

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\rm\underline{\rm Block\ 1)}\ Asymptotic\ \ \ \ \ \ \underline{B1ock\ 2)}\ Computational,\ \ \ \underline{Block\ 3)}\ Finite\ set\ of}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\rm\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ homogenization,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ homogenization,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ inclusions.}

(3.10)

Blocks 1 and 2 are applied to periodic structure composites (see Introduction). Block 3 corresponds to one and the finite set of inclusions for either the finite-size sample
or the infinite matrix with a finite set of inclusions (in such a case, the problem can be solved by either the volume integral equation methods or boundary integral equation one, see for references, e.g.
[
80
]
).
The key distinction between CMic and AMic lies in the reliance on DNS, which sets computational methods apart from analytical ones. This differentiation is not based on the conventional definitions of “analytical” and “computational” but instead arises from the specific techniques employed to model and estimate material properties.
It should be mentioned that the classification of AMic (
3.2
) and CMic (
3.2
) hold also for PM (see for details
[
96
]
).

A key concept in analytical micromechanics is the GIE, which precisely relates random fields at a given point to those in its surroundings.
Derivation of the GIE begins with the static governing equation for displacement, which follows a Navier-like form

\displaystyle\nabla{{}^{L}\!}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=-\nabla{{}^{L}\!}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}).

(3.11)

Various forms of GIEs, ranked by increasing generality, are detailed in
[
64
]
and reproduced in
[
80
]
(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in w
):

\displaystyle{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}({\bf x})\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}^{w\Gamma}+\int{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})~d{\bf y},

(3.12)

\displaystyle{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}({\bf x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}\rangle({\bf x})+\int{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})[^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})-\overline{\mbox{$\langle$}}{{}^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})}]~d{\bf y},

(3.13)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}({\bf x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}\rangle({\bf x})+\int[{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})-\underline{\mbox{$\langle$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})]~d{\bf y},

(3.14)

\mbox{$\langle$}(\cdot)\mbox{$\rangle$}
and
\mbox{$\langle$}(\cdot)\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
are the statistical averages introduced in Subsection 2.4. Hereafter, one introduces the infinite body Green’s function
{\bf G}^{(0)}
of the Navier equation with homogeneous elastic modului
{}^{L}\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}}^{(0)}
:

\nabla\left\{{{}^{L}\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}}^{(0)}[{\nabla\otimes{\bf G}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})}]}\right\}

=-\mbox{\boldmath$\delta$}\delta({\bf x}),

of order
O\big(\int|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|^{1-d}d|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|\big)
as
|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|\to\infty
and vanishing at infinity (
|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|\to\infty
). The Green’s tensors for the strains is used

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\nabla\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\bf G$}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}).

Comment 3.1.
The historical development of the GIE from Eq. (
3.12
) to Eq. (
3.14
), with key milestones (
[
384
]
,
[
412
]
,
[
254
]
,
[
361
]
), is outlined in
[
80
]
. So, the integral in Eq.
(
3.12
) has no absolute convergence whereas in Eq. (
3.13
) (proposed in
[
384
]
at
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\equiv
const.) there are no difficulties connected with the asymptotic behavior of the generalized
functions
\bf U
at infinity (as
|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}|^{1-d}
) and there is no need
to postulate either the shape or the size of the integration domain
w

[
220
]
,
[
173
]
,

[
245
]
,
[
246
]
(see also
[
428
]
) or to
resort to either regularization
[
249
]
,
[
251
]
,
[
269
]
,
[
270
]
,
[
277
]
or renormalization
[
106
]
,
[
342
]
, or to consider an auxiliary problem with mixed boundary conditions
[
49
]
,
[
50
]
of integrals (
3.12
)
that are divergent at infinity
[
466
]
.
The shift of the statistical average bracket from
\overline{\mbox{$\langle$}}
(
3.13
) to
\underline{\mbox{$\langle$}}
(
3.14
) marks the emergence
the second background of micromechanics (called also Computational Analytical Micromechanics , CAM)
[
80
]
, a pivotal advancement in micromechanics since Rayleigh’s first intuitive formulation of GIE (
3.13
)
[
384
]
(at
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\equiv
const.).

3.3
Modeling of one inclusion inside the infinite matrix

We consider the equilibrium equation for an infinite
R^{d}
(
d=1,2,3
) homogeneous peridynamic medium
(
2.16
) subjected to the body force with compact support
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
2.4
).

\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{(0)},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})={\bf 0}.

(3.15)

A prescribed displacement
\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(called the
effective displacement field
) corresponds to the self-equilibrated body-force density
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
. In particular, a case
\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
corresponding to the homogeneous effective strain is

\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\ \ \ \overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}={\rm const.}

Let us consider a wacrodomain
w
with one inclusion
v_{i}
subjected to the prescribed effective field loading
\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
corresponding to the body force with compact support
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
2.4
). Then, Eq.
(
2.6
) for a general peridynamic operator
\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}
can be presented as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}^{(0)}(\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}).

(3.16)

The main advantage of the representation (
3.16
) (see also p. 774 in
[
80
]
) is that it avoids the challenges associated with the fuzzy boundaries that are characteristic of nonlocal theories (see, e.g.,
[
146
]
,
[
257
]
,
[
323
]
,
[
414
]
). Equation (
3.16
) simplifies these challenges by eliminating the need for volumetric boundary conditions and sidestepping the difficulties of properly imposing surface effects (see for references and details, e.g.
[
394
]
,
[
460
]
,
[
494
]
, and
[
42
]
