, Chapter 14) in nonlocal models. This approach contributes to a more tractable and efficient formulation for analyzing materials under nonlocal influences.

Hereafter for the contraction a solution of Eq. (
3.16
) for nonlinear elastic case,
the
perturbators
and field concentrations are defined in the reduced form
(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in R^{d}
)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})-\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}),

(3.17)

where we introduce the substitutions

(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$})\leftrightarrow\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$},\ \ \ (\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$},\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$})\leftrightarrow\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$},\ \ \ [\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},(\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})]\leftrightarrow\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}.

(3.18)

The elements of the doublet
\vartheta
correspond to the variables on the left-hand side of Eq. (
3.18
),
the elements of the doublet
\zeta
correspond to the effective fields on the right-hand sides of the definitions, whereas
the arguments
\bf x
and
(\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
of the doublet
\bf z
correspond to the parameters
\bf u
and
\eta
, respectively.
The superindeces
θζ
of the perturbators
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}
correspond to the variables in the left-hand side
\vartheta
and right-hand side
\zeta
, respectively.
In particular,
the operators
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{k}^{uu}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{k},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}})
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{k}^{u\varepsilon}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{k},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}})

(called the
perturbators
of the displacement)
have the physical interpretation of perturbations
introduced by a single heterogeneity
v_{k}
in the infinite matrix subjected to the
effective fields
\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
and

\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
, respectively, where at first no restrictions are imposed on the inhomogeneities of the effective fields
\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
and
\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
.

Let us consider two inclusions
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
placed in an infinite homogeneous matrix and subjected to the inhomogeneous field
\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$},\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$};\ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$},\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$};\ \ [\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},(\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})]=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$};\ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
).
We can transform Eq. (
3.5
) into the following ones (
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}\in v_{i}^{l}
)

\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})-\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})-\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},{\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}}_{i,j}):=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},{\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}}_{i,j})

(3.19)

defining the perturbator
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},{\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}}_{i,j})
which can be found
by any numerical method analogously to the operator
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}}_{i})
(
3.17
).
It should be mentioned that the operators
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},{\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}}_{i,j})
and

\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},{\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}}_{i,j})
(
3.19
) act on the effective fields
\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
at
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i},v_{j}
, respectively, and the kernel of the operator
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i,j}
can be decomposed
(
K=I,J)
:

\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{I\theta\zeta}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{J\theta\zeta}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}),\ \ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{K\theta\zeta}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})V_{k}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}),

(3.20)

where one follows Mura’s tensor indicial notation (see for details
[
80
]
).
The double superindices
θζ

is used analogously to the double superindices
uu
and

uε

in Eqs. (
3.17
).

Similarly, the effective field perturbators
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i,j}

and
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}^{\theta\zeta\infty}_{i,j}

can be defined;
they describe the perturbation of the effective field

\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})-\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})
introduced by both the heterogeneity
v_{j}
(interacting with
v_{i}
) and the fictitious inclusion
with the response operator
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{(0)}
and eigenfield
\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}_{1}^{\rm fict}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})
corresponding to the field in the remote inclusion
v_{j}
(without interaction with
v_{i}
) (
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}\in v_{j},\ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i},\ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
)

\displaystyle\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})-\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}_{i,j}^{\theta\zeta}(\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{i,j})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})

(3.21)

\displaystyle\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})-\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}_{i,j}^{\theta\zeta\infty}(\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{i,j})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}),

(3.22)

(see for details
[
80
]
).

Comment 3.2.

Estimation of the perturabator
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})
(
3.17
)
is, in fact, a basic problem of micromechanics (see Introduction) for one inclusion inside the infinite homogeneous
matrix.
In the PM, estimation of the perturabator
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})
(
3.17
) was considered by four different methods (see

[
80
]
,
[
76
]
) for the linear bond-based medium with the same horizon
l_{\delta}
in both the inclusion and matrix.
The generalization to the linear state-based model as well as to the multiphysics coupled problem
(see the LM applications in
[
64
]
) are straightforward.
The popular discretization methods for the solution of PD equations (see for references
[
121
]
,
[
122
]
,

[
310
]
) are the meshfree method with one-point Gaussian quadrature referring
to it as “meshfree PD”
[
419
]
(see also the solutions for 1D case

[

]
,
[

]
,

[

]
,
[

]
,
[

]
,

]

and 2D case

[
43
]
,
[
226
]
,
[
292
]
,
[
392
]
), finite element methods (FEM,
[
111
]
,
[

]
,
[
387
]
,
[

]
,

[

]
,
[

]
),
[

]
), quadrature and collocation approaches
[

]

]

Adaptive algorithm (see, e.g.,

) using a multi-grid approach with fine grid spacing only in critical regions
is designed in multi-adaptive approach (

to dynamically switch both the discretization scheme

Comment 3.3.

The significantly less trivial phenomenon is another limiting case of
CCM-PD coupling, usually small areas of a domain
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which might be affected by
the presence of discontinuities is described with a PD model, whereas the remaining
parts of the system are described through a more efficient CCM model. The goal of Local-to-Nonlocal (LtN) coupling (see
[
41
]
,
[
123
]
,

[
363
]
,

[
496
]
,
[
497
]
,
[
500
]
,
[
509
]
) is to alleviate the computational burden
by combining the computational efficiency of PDEs with the accuracy of nonlocal models
under the assumption that the location of nonlocal effects can be preliminary prescribed (it looks like a localized plasticity model in the vicinity of inclusions in the LM of CMs, see pp. 556-565
[
80
]
).
It would be interesting to estimate
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})
for the different nonlocal models in the phases (up to the vanishing length scale
l_{\delta}/a\to 0
in some areas).

3.4
General integral equations (GIEs)

In this subsection, at first, we consider peridynamic statistically homogeneous CMs
occupying the space
R^{d}
(
d=1,2,3)
. We consider so-called the
general integral equation
(GIE) connecting the random fields at the point
being considered and all surrounding points. At first, the loading by the body force with a compact support

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
2.4
), the direct summations of all surrounding perturbators
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})

(
3.5
) exerting on the fixed inclusion
v_{i}
is described by the GIE (called the Additive GIE, AGIE, see for details
[
94
]
)
(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}\in v_{i}
)

\displaystyle\langle{\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}^{b(0)}({\bf z})+\int\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}|v_{1},{\bf x}_{1})d{\bf x}_{j}

(3.23)

with the deterministic fields
\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}^{b(0)}({\bf z})
produced by the body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
in the infinite homogeneous matrix.
The term
Additive
GIE is used similarly to
Additive
Manufacturing because the perturbations in Eq. (
3.23
) are directly added without any renormalization terms, such as those in Eq. (
3.24
).
A centering of Eqs. (
3.16
) is considered which performs a subtraction from both sides of Eq. (
3.23
) of their statistical averages. It leads to the GIE

\displaystyle\langle{\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}\mbox{$\rangle$}({\bf z})+\int\big[\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}|v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})

(3.24)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j})\big]d{\bf x}_{j},

which is more general and valid for any inhomogeneous
\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}\rangle({\bf z})
while Eqs. (
3.23
) are only correct for the the body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
with a compact support. Owing to the centering of Eq. (
3.16
), Eq. (
3.24
)
contains the renormalizing term
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j})
providing an absolute convergence
of the integrals involved in Eqs. (
3.24
).
The derivation of the exact form of the GIE (
3.24
) has a long and intricate history, particularly in the context of LM of CMs (see
[
62
]
,
[
64
]
for references and details). The first GIE was heuristically proposed by Lord Rayleigh
[
384
]
and was later rigorously established in 1977 (see
[
80
]
).
A second GIE in the framework of LM, along with its generalizations to strongly and weakly nonlocal linear heterogeneous materials, was introduced between 2010 and 2022 (see for references and details
[
80
]
). However, in all prior developments, specific instances of GIE (
3.24
) were obtained through intricate multistep procedures, constrained by particular linear constitutive relations governing the phases of the material. In contrast, a more general form of GIE (
3.24
), applicable to CMs with strongly and weakly nonlocal as well as nonlinear phase properties, was derived directly from the newly formulated AGIE (
3.23
) via a single-step centering operation. This advancement eliminates the need for case-specific formulations and significantly broadens the applicability of the GIE and AGIE framework.

After a statistical average of Eqs. (
3.23
) and (
3.24
), the conditional perturbator
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})|;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{j}
can be expressed through the
explicit perturbator for two interacting heterogeneities subjected to the field
\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{i,j}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\langle\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}}_{i}\rangle(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}^{b(0)}({\bf z})+\int\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i,j}(\mbox{$\langle$}\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{i,j}\mbox{$\rangle$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}|;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})\,d{\bf x}_{j},

(3.25)

\displaystyle\langle\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}}_{i}\rangle(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})\!

\displaystyle=\!

\displaystyle\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}\rangle({\bf z})+\int\bigl\{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i,j}(\mbox{$\langle$}\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{i,j}\mbox{$\rangle$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}|;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})

(3.26)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}^{\theta\zeta\infty}_{i,j}(\mbox{$\langle$}\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{i,j}\mbox{$\rangle$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})\bigl\}d{\bf x}_{j},

where the conditional probability density
\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}|;v_{1},{\bf x}_{1})
for one fixed inclusion was used
(see for details
[
80
]
);
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}|;v_{1},{\bf x}_{1})
stands
a perturbation
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})
at the fixad inclusion
v_{1}\not=v_{j}
.
The deterministic field
\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}^{b(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})
(
3.25
) produced by the forcing term
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
in the infinite homogeneous matrix.
Equations (
3.25
) and (
3.26
) are central to the overall formulation, capturing the material’s response under the applied effective field loading. These equations are constructed based on numerical solutions for one or two inclusions, and they incorporate statistically averaged fields
\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{i}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
and
\mbox{$\langle$}\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{i,j}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

to represent the overall macroscopic behavior of the composite or heterogeneous material.
There are a couple of fundamental advantages of Eqs. (
3.25
) and (
3.26
).
So, these equations have no Green functions and, moreover, the most intriguing feature of Eqs. (
3.25
) and (
3.26
)
is the absence of a constitutive law in these equations. As we can see, Eqs. (
3.25
) and (
3.26
) for the peridynamic micromechanics coincides with the corresponding equation for the LM CMs (see
[
64
]
). It means that the same equation can be used for both the peridynamic CMs and locally elastic CMs.
The GIEs (
3.25
) and (
3.26
) proposed are adapted to the straightforward generalizations of corresponding methods of local thermoelastic micromechanics (see, e.g.,
[
54
]
,
[
62
]
,
[
64
]
) to their peridynamic counterparts.

Comment 3.4.

The analytical micromechanics (Amic) formulation (
3.2
) is generally derived from the solutions to Eqs. (
3.25
) and (
3.26
), along with their specific cases. In particular, within the LM framework, the perturbations
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})
are determined using various methods like FEA (
[
99
]
,

[
100
]
,
[
101
]
,
[
102
]
), VIE method (
[
55
]
,
[
56
]
), BIE approach (
[
60
]
,
[
67
]
,
[
70
]
,
[
72
]
).
These solutions are then substituted into Eq. (
3.14
) to estimate the effective moduli
{}^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{*}
and stress concentration factors.
The classical hypotheses for the LM, such as the Effective Field Hypothesis (EFH) (
3.1
) and its extension, closing assumptions along with the assumption of ellipsoidal symmetry, have been analyzed in detail (
[
80
]
). These hypotheses are fundamental to the classical LM methods, including the Effective Field Method (EFM), the Mori-Tanaka Method (MTM), and the Multiparticle Effective Field Method (MEFM).
The GIE (
3.14
) enhances the accuracy of local field estimations, even correcting the sign of predictions within inclusions (
[
80
]
).
Equation (
3.14
) has been extended to address a variety of other problems within the LM framework: thermoelasticity (
[
69
]
,
[
70
]
,
[
100
]
,
[
101
]
,
[
102
]
), coupled problems (
[
62
]
,
[
64
]
), and wave propagation (
[
62
]
,
[
64
]
), and infiltration in porous media
[
66
]
. It has also been adapted for CMs with both strongly nonlocal (e.g., strain-type, displacement-type, and peridynamics) and weakly nonlocal (e.g., strain-gradient, stress-gradient, and higher-order models) phase properties, as discussed in Chapter 14 of
[
80
]
.
However, a key limitation of all these GIEs and their extensions (considered in the papers referred to in Subsection 3.4) is that they remain linear in relation to the primary unknown variable. To the author’s knowledge, there are no existing nonlinear versions of the GIE (
3.24
) and the AGIE (
3.23
)—whether for LM or PM—that directly or implicitly account for nonlinear constitutive phase properties.
In the following section, the author proposes a solution to the nonlinear AGIEs (
3.23
), offering a potential way to address this gap.

4
Solution of nonlinear AGIEs for random structure CMs

4.1
Iterative solution of AGIE

The effective field hypothesis, which serves as the fundamental approximation in numerous micromechanical methods, is mathematically formulated by Eq. (
3.8
) (for details, see
[
54
]
).
To achieve closure of the AGIE (
3.25
), the following additional hypothesis is introduced:

Hypothesis H2a)
.
Each pair of inclusions
v_{j}
and
v_{j}

is subjected to the inhomogeneous field

\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
, and statistical average

\mbox{$\langle$}\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{i,j}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
is defined by the formula
(\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$},\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}
)

\displaystyle\mbox{$\langle$}\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{i,j}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

(4.1)

at
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{k},\ k=i,j
.

The hypothesis
H2a
, when reformulated in terms of the strain fields
\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
for
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}
, represents a conventional closure assumption (for further details, see
[
54
]
,
[
247
]
,
[
466
]
).
This assumption reduces to the ”quasicrystalline” approximation introduced by Lax
[
288
]
, which neglects the pairwise interactions between heterogeneities and assumes spatial uniformity of the effective fields.

Hypothesis H2b, “quasi-crystalline” approximation
.

It is supposed that the mean value of the effective field at a point

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}
does not depend on the field inside other heterogeneities

v_{j}\not=v_{i}
,
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{k},\ (k=i,j)
:

\displaystyle\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}|v_{i},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i};v_{j},\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle{\rm const.}

(4.3)

Acceptance of hypothesis
H2a
closes the system (
3.25
) in the following forms,

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\langle\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}}_{i}\rangle(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}^{b(0)}({\bf z})+\!\!\int\![\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}^{I\theta\zeta}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{i}\mbox{$\rangle$})\!+\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}^{J\theta\zeta}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{j}\mbox{$\rangle$})]\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}|;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})\,d{\bf x}_{j},

(4.4)

were a decomposition
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i,j}=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}^{I\theta\zeta}_{i,j}+\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}^{J\theta\zeta}_{i,j}

was introduced analogously to Eq. (
3.20
).
The integral equation (
4.4
) can be solved using an iterative approach based on a recursive formula

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\langle\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}}_{i}^{[n+1]}\rangle(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}^{b(0)}({\bf z})+\int[\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}^{I\theta\zeta}_{i,j}(\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}^{[n]}_{i}\mbox{$\rangle$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}^{J\theta\zeta}_{i,j}(\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}^{[n]}_{j}\mbox{$\rangle$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})]

(4.5)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\!\!\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}|;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})d{\bf x}_{j},

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}^{[n+1]}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal A$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i}(\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}^{[n+1]}_{i}\mbox{$\rangle$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}),

(4.6)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\bar{v}_{i}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}^{[n+1]}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}({\bf x})\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal R$}}_{i}(\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}^{[n+1]}_{i}\mbox{$\rangle$})({\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}).

(4.7)

The iterative solution is constructed with an initial approximation derived as an explicit solution from Equations (
4.3
), within the framework of the effective field hypothesis
H1a
(
3.8
)
(see for details
[
80
]
).

Equation (
4.3
) admits a simplified form under Hypothesis Hb2, as shown in Equations (
4.2
), which are also utilized in the LM method (see
[
54
]
).
However, a direct generalization of Lax’s “quasicrystalline” approximation is possible (see Equation (
4.2
)) when the assumptions in (
4.2
) are relaxed, allowing the statistical average of the perturbed strain field,
\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})
,
to vary spatially rather than remain constant, i.e.,

\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})\not\equiv{\rm const}
for
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}\in v_{k}^{l},

(k=i,j)

\displaystyle\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{j})|;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i}\mbox{$\rangle$}=\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{j})\mbox{$\rangle$}.

(4.8)

This significantly simplifies the problem formulated in Eq. (
4.3
), where, under these conditions, the equation takes a more tractable form

\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i,j}=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}_{i,j}^{\theta\zeta\infty}

(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}
) that reduces Eq. (
4.3
) to

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\langle\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}}_{i}^{[n+1]}\rangle(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}^{b(0)}({\bf z})+\!\!\int\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}^{\theta\zeta\infty}_{i,j}(\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{j}^{[n]}\mbox{$\rangle$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}|;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})\,d{\bf x}_{j}.

(4.9)

The second background of LM, introduced in
[
55
]
,
[
56
]
and mathematically formulated in Eq. (
4.9
), enables the relaxation of fundamental micromechanical assumptions, including the ellipsoidal symmetry hypothesis and the EFH
H1a
.
As a result, novel physical effects have been identified that could not be captured within the classical (first) theoretical framework of micromechanics.

Equations (
4.4
) and (
4.9
) are derived for general nonlinear cases, incorporating both state-based (
2.8
) and bond-based (
2.15
) peridynamic models (PM). In the special case of a linear bond-based PM (
2.24
), these equations simplify previously established results (see
[
84
]
).

4.2
Effective constitutive law and dataset

Equation (
4.9
) is reformulated in terms of displacement fields (
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}
)

\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}^{[n+1]}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}^{b(0)}({\bf x})+\int\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{uu}_{q}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}^{[n]}|;v_{i},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i})\varphi(v_{q},{\bf x}_{q}|;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})d{\bf x}_{q}.

(4.10)

The initial approximation is defined by the driving term, corresponding to the zero-order approximation, given as
\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}^{[0]}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}^{b(0)}({\bf x})
, which itself is expressed by Equation (
3.15
).
The iterative scheme in Eq. (
4.10
) constructs a Neumann series representation for the solution
(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}^{l}
)

\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}^{[n+1]}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}_{i}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}).

(4.11)

This leads to the formulation of the statistical averages of the field within the fixed extended inclusion, specifically the conditional averages for
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}^{l}

\displaystyle\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}_{i}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{i}^{uu}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}_{i}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})),

(4.12)

\displaystyle\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma}(\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}).

(4.13)

The tensor
\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}_{q}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})
(
4.11
)
is an inhomogeneous function of coordinates of the fixed inclusion
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}^{l}
depending on all interecting inclusions (at least at
|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q}|\leq a^{\delta}+l_{\delta}
) whereas in the dilute approximation the corresponding tensor is
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf I$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})
.

Equation (
4.12
) provides a means to compute the expected value of the displacement field over the macroscopic region
\bf X

\langle{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$}):=c^{l(0)}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{l(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})+c^{l(1)}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{l(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})

(4.14)

The statistical averages of the displacement field in the macropoint are expressed through the ensemble averages of displacements at the point
\bf X
within the matrix, denoted as
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{l(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
, and within the inclusions, represented as
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{l(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
.
To establish this framework, we introduce an auxiliary domain
v^{1}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
, which is characterized by the indicator function
V^{1}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
and is bounded by
\partial v^{1}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
, the locus of the centers of translated ellipsoidal regions
v_{q}({\bf 0})
around the fixed macroscopic point point
\bf X
. The domain
v^{1}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
is then obtained as the limiting form of a sequence
v^{0}_{ki}\to v_{q}^{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
,
where
v^{0}_{ki}\to v_{q}^{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
as a given ellipsoid
v_{k}
contracts to the point
\bf X
.
Then
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{l(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
can be estimated as (
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}\in v^{l}_{q})

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!c^{l(1)}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{l(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!c^{l(1)}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}^{b(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})+\!\!\int_{v^{1}_{i}({\bf X})}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!n^{(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q})\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{uu}_{q}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$}\!-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q},\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}_{q}^{u}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}))d{\bf x}_{q}.

(4.15)

Consequently, the statistical expectation of the displacement field, given by Eq. (
4.14
), can be represented in terms of the volume force density distribution within the region
v_{q}^{l}
, where
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}\in v^{l}_{q}

\displaystyle\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}^{b(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})+c^{(0)}\int\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{uu}_{q}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q},\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}_{q}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}))\varphi(v_{q},{\bf x}_{q}|;\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})d{\bf x}_{q}

(4.16)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\!\int_{v^{1}_{i}({\bf X})}n^{(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q})\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{uu}_{q}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q},\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}_{q}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}))d{\bf x}_{q},

In Eq. (
4.16
), the first and second integrals correspond respectively to the first and second terms on the right-hand side of Eq. (
4.14
). Additionally, the function
\varphi(v_{q},{\bf x}_{q}|;\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
vanishes, i.e.,
\varphi(v_{q},{\bf x}_{q}|;\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})=0
, whenever the point
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q}
belongs to the domain
v^{1}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
.

The macroscopic stress field in the effective constitutive law is expressed as

\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

(4.17)

where the first term on the right-hand side simplifies for the linear matrix and depends on the statistical averages
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
:

\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma(0)}(\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
) (
4.16
).
To estimate the average
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
, we first consider the fixed inclusion

v_{q}
centered at
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q}
.
which is estimated using Eq. (
2.17
) with the substitution
This inclusion yields the value

\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma}(\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}})
which is estimated using Equation (
2.17
) with the substitution
\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}}^{(0)}\to\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf f$}}_{1}
as indicated in Eq.
(
3.21
).
The statistical average of the local polarization tensor
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
is then obtained by averaging over the domain

v_{i}^{1}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{v^{1}_{i}({\bf X})}n^{(1)}\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}_{1}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}^{D})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$},

(4.18)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}^{D}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}_{i}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{i}^{uu}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}_{i}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

(4.19)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}_{i}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})-\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{i}^{uu}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}_{i}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}))\Big],

Here,
v_{q}^{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
is defined by Equation (
2.41
), and the statistical average of the displacement field within the inclusion
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{q}
is represented by
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{q}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
, as shown in Equation (
4.15
).
The estimation of the effective micromodulus
[
481
]
, which involves averaging the strain energy of the peridynamic material model under homogeneous boundary conditions, raises certain concerns regarding its validity.

The new nonlocal effective constitutive law, expressed in Equation (
4.17
), bears a strong resemblance to the previously established nonlocal effective constitutive law

\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})={{}^{L}}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{(0)}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{$\langle$}^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}),\ \ \ ^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}):={{}^{L}}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}).

(4.20)

This is analogous to the locally elastic constitutive law (
2.2
) at the microlevel. However, the first term

{{}^{L}}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{(0)}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

on the right-hand side of Eq. (
4.20
) corresponds to a general inhomogeneous strain field, where
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\not={\bf 0}
. On the other hand, the term
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
from Eq. (
4.17
) simplifies to
\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{(0)}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
only when the strain field
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
is homogeneous, i.e.,
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\equiv
cons.

Numerical analysis was conducted for a 1D statistically homogeneous random structure bar
[
83
]
,
[
84
]
under a self-equilibrated body force condition (
2.3
), where
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=0
for

|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|>B^{b}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
for
|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|\leq B^{b}
;
c^{b}=0
. The engineering approach, utilizing the scale separation hypothesis (
2.38
2
), follows this scheme: first, estimate
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{*}
by CAM (see Comment 3.4 and a generalized EFM,
[
80
]
) under homogeneous boundary conditions (
2.40
), and then evaluate
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}^{\rm EA}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
using Eqs. (
2.1
)–(
2.3
) with the substitution

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}\to\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{*}
. This leads to a monotonically increasing displacement field
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}^{\rm EA}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(represented by the solid curve 4 in Fig. 1). In Fig. 1, the parameters
l_{\delta}/a=1
and
c^{(1)}=0.5
are fixed, while
B^{b}/a
takes values of
0.25,\ 0.375,\ 0.5
(shown in curves 1, 2, and 3, respectively).
As observed in Fig. 1, the simultaneous inclusion of the peridynamic constitutive equation (
2.5
) and the inhomogeneity of the body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
results in strongly nonmonotonic distributions of the displacement field
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
, which differ from
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}^{\rm EA}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
by a factor of 9 for curve 1. Due to nonlocal effects, the range of long-range action is restricted not only by
|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|\leq B^{b}
, but by a domain
|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|\leq a^{\rm l-r}

(a^{\rm l-r}\approx 3B^{b}
), meaning that the problem domain

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in R^{1}
is effectively reduced to a finite domain, avoiding the inconsistencies previously mentioned in
[
82
]
.
This implies that the domain size
|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|\leq a^{\rm l-r}\approx 3B^{b}
(at the considered scale ratios
a/a^{b}/l_{\delta}
) requires stabilization of the displacement fields
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\approx
const. (for
|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|>a^{\rm l-r}
), which significantly exceeds the combined scale of the body force
B^{b}
and the horizon
l_{\delta}
(a synergistic effect).
The parameter
a^{\rm l-r}
should be further learned.
Although the term “RVE” ( see for details Subsection 8.3) was not directly employed, the domain
|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|\leq a^{\rm l-r}
in fact represents the RVE for
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
and this RVE depends on the scale ratios
a/a^{b}/l_{\delta}
.
In generalizing this phenomenon for an arbitrary body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
as given in Eq. (
2.4
), we can define the RVE as the region for which there exists a characteristic size
B^{\rm RVE}
such that

\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}^{\infty}\equiv{\rm const}.,\ \ {\rm for}\ \ \forall\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\geq B^{\rm RVE},

(4.21)

where this equality holds within a prescribed tolerance.

For the linear bond-based model of constituents, the governing equations (
4.14
)–(
4.17
) reduce to the corresponding relations derived in
[
83
]
,
[
84
]
.
For statistically homogeneous composite materials with locally elastic constituent phases, the nonlocal effective operator derived in
[
321
]
incorporates microstructural information via the Green’s function of the reference matrix medium (
2.5
), allowing for a rigorous treatment of the effective response under arbitrary body force distributions. However, the extension of this methodology to PM, where nonlocal interactions play a fundamental role, is not straightforward due to the absence of classical differential operators.
Potential applications of micromechanical modeling of composites subjected to body forces with compact support—such as those induced by localized thermal loading (e.g., laser heating, as discussed in
[
480
]
,
[
234
]
, and
[
484
]
)—were analyzed in
[
96
]
. These studies emphasize the necessity of incorporating spatially varying internal forces within a nonlocal framework to accurately capture the mechanical response under external stimuli.

For an arbitrary body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
with compact support, we have derived formal representations for the effective macroscopic and microscopic fields in a composite material (CM) with a random microstructure. Specifically, we consider the macroscopic displacement

\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
and stress
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
, as well as their corresponding microscopic counterparts
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
and
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
, where the subscript
i
denotes quantities evaluated within a representative inclusion
v_{i}
.
For computational implementation and subsequent applications, we construct a dataset of effective parameters for various realizations of the applied body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
, expressed as (
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in R^{d})

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm r}

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N},

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm r}_{k}

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\{\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\!\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\!\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}_{ik}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\!\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}}_{ik}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\}.

(4.22)

Here, each realization
k
th corresponds to a specific body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k}
, for which the macroscopic displacement
\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
and stress
\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
fields are computed, along with the local inclusion-scale displacement

\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}_{ik}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}):=\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})
and stress
\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}}_{ik}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}):=\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})
.
The macroscopic coordinate
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in R^{d}
spans the domain of the homogenized CM, while the local coordinate
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}\in v_{i}
resides within the representative inclusion
v_{i}
.
This dataset

{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm r}
forms a basis for data-driven modeling, enabling efficient retrieval and interpolation of effective response functions for new realizations of
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k}
in a computationally efficient manner.

5
Periodic structure CMs

Equations (
3.23
) and (
3.25
) were derived under the general assumption of arbitrary probability densities
\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j})
and the conditional probability density
\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}|;v_{1},{\bf x}_{1})
, which characterize the statistical distribution of the microstructural inclusions within the CM. However, in subsequent developments, we specialize these representations to periodic structure CMs, where the probability densities
\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j})
and
\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}|;v_{1},{\bf x}_{1})
take deterministic forms, given explicitly as sums of Dirac delta functions (
2.36
) localized at the periodic grid points
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{\alpha}\in\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}
, corresponding to the structured arrangement of inclusions.

Consequently, the integral relations expressed in Eqs. (
4.11
)–(
4.13
) undergo a transformation to a discrete summation form reflecting the periodic nature of the material structure

\displaystyle\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}^{[n+1]}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}_{i}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),

(5.1)

\displaystyle{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}_{i}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{i}^{uu}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}_{i}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})),

(5.2)

\displaystyle{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma}({\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}).

(5.3)

Despite their formal similarity in appearance, these transformed equations (
5.1
)–(
5.3
) and the corresponding set of equations (
4.11
)–(
4.13
) are conceptually distinct. Equations (
4.11
)–(
4.13
) inherently involve ensemble-averaged effective parameters.
Equations (
5.1
)–(
5.3
), though similar in structure, pertain specifically to periodic composites and thus rely on a deterministic framework where the microstructure
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{\alpha}\in\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}
is predefined.

To perform the statistical averaging of Eqs. (
5.1
)–(
5.3
), we employ the translated averaging technique (see

[
84
]
,
[
95
]
,
[
97
]
for details),
which is based on shifting the periodic grid
\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}_{0}
with corresponding inclusion centers by a translation vector
\chi
, generating a new grid

\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}_{\bf\chi}
. The body force field
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
remains fixed throughout this transformation. Mathematically, we consider a
\chi
, leading to a corresponding transformation in the material properties, the characteristic functions of inclusions, and the displacement field:

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$},\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}_{0}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$}),\ \ V_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$})=V_{i0}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$}),

(5.4)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{0}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$})\not\equiv\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}_{0}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$}),

(5.5)

where the inequality (
5.5
2
) holds because
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
is fixed.
For each translation
\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$}\in\mbox{$\cal V$}_{\rm\bf x}
, the corresponding displacement field
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$})
is determined by solving the governing equations (
2.5
) or (
2.6
). This ensemble of solutions allows us to define the macroscopic (or effective) displacement field by averaging over all possible translations
\chi
, ensuring a statistical homogenization of the material response across different realizations of the periodic microstructure. The macroscopic displacement field is thus given by:

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mbox{$\langle$}\{\cdot\}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})={1\over\overline{\mbox{$\cal V$}}_{\bf x}}\int_{{\cal V}_{\rm\bf x}}\{\cdot,\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$}\}~d\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$},\ \mbox{$\langle$}\{\cdot\}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{l(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})={1\over\overline{\mbox{$\cal V$}}_{\bf x}}\int_{{\cal V}_{\rm\bf x}}\{\cdot,\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$}\}V_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$})~d\mbox{\boldmath$\chi$}.

(5.6)

The averages in Eqs. (
5.6
) represent ensemble averaging over all possible translated realizations of a periodic microstructure, where translations
\chi
are uniformly distributed over the periodicity cell. This translated averaging applies to periodic CMs with any phase constitutive laws and any inhomogeneous loading.
A special case, similar to (
5.6
1
), was introduced in asymptotic homogenization by
[
426
]
and
[
14
]
for periodic media under periodic loading. Interestingly, the statistical formulations in Eq. (
5.6
2
) resemble a student probability problem: inclusion
v_{i}
is randomly ”dropped” onto a fixed point
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in R^{d}
. However, in (
5.6
2
), the inclusion belongs to a randomly translated periodic grid
\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}_{\chi}
.

Applying the translated averaging operation (
5.6
) to Eq. (
5.2
) and Eq. (
4.11
) yields the following expressions
(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}\in v^{l}_{q})

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{l(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}^{b(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})+{1\over\overline{\mbox{$\cal V$}}_{\bf x}}\int_{{\cal V}_{\rm\bf x}}V(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q})\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{uu}_{q}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q},\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}_{q}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q}))d{\bf x}_{q},

(5.7)

\displaystyle\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}^{b(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})+{c^{l(0)}\over\overline{\mbox{$\cal V$}}_{\bf x}}\int_{{\cal V}_{\rm\bf x}}V^{l(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q})\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{uu}_{q}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q},\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}_{q}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q}))d{\bf x}_{q},

(5.8)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\!{c^{l(1)}\over\overline{\mbox{$\cal V$}}_{\bf x}}\int_{{\cal V}_{\rm\bf x}}V^{l(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q})\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{uu}_{q}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q},\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}_{q}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q}))d{\bf x}_{q},

which correspond to Eqs. (
4.15
) and (
4.16
), respectively,
for the general case of the conditional probability density

\varphi(v_{q},{\bf x}_{q}|;\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
.

The averages
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{l(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
(
5.7
) and
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
(
5.8
)
depend on the macrocoordinates
\bf X
and are not tied to a specific periodic grid
\Lambda
.
In a similar manner, the effective fields
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{l(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
and
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
can be found.
Translation averaging of constitutive lau (
3.5
) with linear matrix (
3.1
) will lead to formally identical Eq. (
4.17
)

\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma(0)}(\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

(5.9)

with

\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma(0)}(\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
) (
5.8
).
I.e., the term
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma}(\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}}^{(0)})\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
4.13
) is equivalent to the stresses in a pure matrix

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{(0)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}})
produced by the total displacement
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
5.8
).

For estimation of an average
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}^{\sigma}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}_{1},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
3.6
), we consider, at first, the fixed inclusion

v_{q}
with the center
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q}
. This inclusion produces value

{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}^{\sigma}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}_{1},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q})
.
Then
a statistical average of the local polarization tensor
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})
is also obtained by averaging over
the domain
v_{i}^{1}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{1\over\overline{\mbox{$\cal V$}}_{\bf x}}\int_{{\cal V}_{\rm\bf x}}V^{l(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\sigma}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}_{1}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}^{D})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf X$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$},

(5.10)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}^{D}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}_{i}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{i}^{uu}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}_{i}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

(5.11)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}_{i}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})-\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{i}^{uu}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},\widehat{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}_{i}^{ub}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}))\Big],

where one used the representation for the displacement field in
the inclusion
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{q}
:
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
5.2
).

Analogously to the dataset
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
(
4.2
) for random structure CMs, we can define a dataset
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm p}
for periodic structure CMs

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm p}

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm p}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N},

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm p}_{k}

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\{\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\!\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\!\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{l(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\!\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\},

(5.12)

where each effective parameter is computed for a given
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k}
using Eqs. (
5.6
) during the offline stage for the
k
-th realization. The macrocoordinates
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in R^{d}
define a coarser dataset
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm p}
(
5
), which contains less detailed information than
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
(
4.2
). The latter depends not only on the macrocoordinates
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in R^{d}
but also on the local coordinates
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}\in v_{i}
within the representative inclusion
v_{i}
, providing finer-scale resolution.

Thus, the macroscopic stresses in the effective constitutive law (
5.9
)
depends on the statistical average
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in R^{d}
) (
5.8
) and

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})
(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}\in v^{l}_{q}
) (
5.2
), respectively. However, these displacement fields

\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
5.8
) and

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})
(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}\in v^{l}_{q}
) (
5.2
) are not the prime variables and depend on the body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
.
The estimation of effective constitutive law in the next subsection is based on the solution of the GIE (
3.25
) rather than (
3.26
).

Comment 5.1.

To the best of the author’s knowledge, the method of reducing DNS to smoothed (effective) parameters (
5
) is novel. Furthermore, a critical aspect in the analysis of periodic structure CMs is the appropriate selection of periodic boundary conditions (PBC) (
2.39
), (
2.40
) and volumetric periodic boundary conditions (VPBC) (
2.37
), (
2.38
) at the interface of unit cells (UCs), which link the field distributions between adjacent UCs.
The established PBC (
2.39
) and VPBC (
2.37
) definitively correspond to both homogeneous remote loading (
2.28
) or (
2.29
) and the zero body force condition
\mathbf{b}(\mathbf{x})\equiv\mathbf{0}
(
). However, for a general body force case (
2.4
), both PBC (
2.39
) and VPBC (
2.37
) become incorrect (see Subsection 2.3). Nevertheless, enforcing any PBC (
2.39
) and VPBC (
2.37
) is unnecessary if DNS is utilized to estimate Eqs. (
5.6
) on a representative volume element (RVE, see Section 7) (possibly consisting of multiple UCs) rather than on a single UC.
The size of the RVE (see Comment 4.13) acts as a controlled parameter that must be determined with a prescribed tolerance. Although the integrands in Eqs. (
5.6
) are computed through DNS within CMic, the combination of Eqs. (
5.6
) with the RVE concept facilitates the construction of the dataset
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm p}
. Consequently, the method (
5.6
) is referred to as CAM’s version for periodic structure CMs.

Comment 5.2.

In Sections 3 and 4, we analyzed matrix random structure composites (CMs) with GIE (
3.24
) and AGIE (
3.23
), which apply to the first group, where a continuous matrix phase contains isolated inhomogeneities. For periodic structure CMs, translation averaging (
5.6
) is used without microtopology restrictions. The second group
[
192
]
includes skeletal, percolated, or laminated composites, where at least two phases form a monolithic frame, such as metallic foams or cellulose-reinforced polymers.
Cellulose-reinforced polymers

[
193
]
, metallic wool,
and glass wool have both a network connecting them to the penetrated structure, and
also an incomplete network with only one connection to the network tree.
The third group involves composites where the phases are contiguous but not interconnected, like polycrystalline metals with different crystallographic orientations, forming heterogeneous materials. Adjusting the constituent concentration can move a composite from one group to another. All periodic structure CMs discussed enable the creation of the dataset
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm p}
(
5
).

Comment 5.3.
The effective macroscopic parameter datasets,
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm p}
, corresponding to the scales
B^{b}
(
2.3
) and
|\Omega_{00}|
, respectively, are functionally independent of the microstructural details of the composite material. Specifically, they do not encode explicit dependencies on the two-point probability distribution
\varphi(v_{q},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{q}|;v_{i},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i})
or the grid
\Lambda
, nor do they retain information regarding the methods used to evaluate the reference fields in Eqs. (
4.11
)–(
4.19
) and (
5.1
)–(
5.11
).
Furthermore, Eq. (
3.24
) is obtained from Eq. (
3.23
) via a centering transformation, thereby making it a particularized form of the latter. However, Eq. (
3.23
) holds a significant computational advantage over Eq. (
3.24
) as it provides the foundation for constructing the dataset
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
, which is instrumental in the development of machine learning (ML) and neural network (NN) methodologies—particularly surrogate operators (see Subsection 7). These ML-based techniques facilitate the generation of surrogate operators for arbitrary macroscopic loading conditions, whether specified in terms of the macroscopic displacement field
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
or the body force distribution
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
. Importantly, this approach circumvents the need to explicitly solve the micro-scale problem dictated by Eq. (
3.24
), thereby significantly reducing computational complexity.

6
Estimation of field fluctuations and effective energy-based criteria

In nonlinear LM problems, a key challenge is estimating the second moment of phase fields (
\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$},\ \mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}
;
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v^{(i)}
):

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}\otimes\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})=\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})\otimes\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})+\Delta^{\pi 2}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),

(6.1)

where
\Delta^{\pi 2}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
is called the dispersion (or the fluctuation field).
Two methods–exact differential analysis and integral equations–yield similar numerical results (
[
54
]
,
[
80
]
). However, using the simplified engineering assumption ⟨
\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}\otimes\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})=\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})\otimes\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})
can result in infinite errors under hydrostatic loading of porous isotropic media.

The integral equation method in LM allows for its extension to PM. Specifically, by considering only binary inclusion interactions, new approximations for the second moment can be derived:

\displaystyle\!\!\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}\otimes\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})\otimes\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})+\!\!\int\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal A$}^{\theta\theta}_{i}*\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{p}^{\theta\zeta}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})|;v_{i},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i}\mbox{$\rangle$}

(6.2)

\displaystyle\otimes

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\cal A$}^{\theta\theta}_{i}*\mbox{$\langle$}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{p}^{\theta\zeta}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})|;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i}\rangle\varphi(v_{p},{\bf x}_{p}\mid;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})d{\bf x}_{p}.

Comment 6.1.

The new equation (
6.3
) is derived for nonlinear PM problems involving the operators
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal A$}^{\theta\theta}_{i}
(
3.2
2
) and
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{p}^{\theta\zeta}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})
(
3.2
1
). Notably, it exactly matches the equation for a medium with remote BCs (
6.3
), (
3.24
) rather than body force loading (
2.4
), (
3.25
). These equations extend previous results from linear bond-based and state-based models
[
90
]
,
[
86
]
and unify them with existing LM formulations
[
54
]
,
[
80
]
. This marks a rare instance where PM methods for second-moment fields (nonlinear problems) inspire new LM techniques for nonlinear analysis. Furthermore, the formulations can be extended, as in
[
86
]
, to include triple-point probability densities
\varphi(v_{p},{\bf x}_{p},v_{q},{\bf x}_{q}|;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})
for greater accuracy.

Equation (
6.3
) can be simplified for linear operators
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal A$}^{\theta\theta}_{i}
(
3.2
2
) and
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{p}^{\theta\zeta}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})
(
3.2
1
) within the EFH framework
H1a
(
3.8
), enabling its application to derive effective energy-based criteria:

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\otimes\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\rangle_{i}({\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\rangle_{i}({\bf z})\otimes\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\rangle_{i}({\bf z})+\Delta^{\eta 2}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}),

(6.3)

where

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\Delta^{\eta 2}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int[\mbox{$\mathbb{A}$}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}]\otimes[\mbox{$\mathbb{A}$}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}]\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}\mid;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})~d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j},

(6.4)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\mbox{$\mathbb{A}$}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf A$}^{\eta\varepsilon}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})[\mbox{\boldmath$\bf J$}^{I\varepsilon\varepsilon}_{i,j}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf D$}^{0}_{i}+\mbox{\boldmath$\bf J$}^{J\varepsilon\varepsilon}_{i,j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})\mbox{\boldmath$\bf D$}^{0}_{j}].

(6.5)

Here
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf A$}^{\eta\varepsilon}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})=\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf A$}}_{i}^{u\varepsilon}[\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}]\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\mbox{$\rangle$}
(
\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}=\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
whereas

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf D$}^{0}_{i}
is the effective field concentration factor
\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf D$}^{0}_{i}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}
(see for details
[
86
]
).

Equation (
6.3
) serves as a “elementary block” for developing various effective energy-based criteria. For instance, in the peridynamic approach to fatigue cracking,
[
359
]
estimated the energy release rate by analyzing the micropotentials governing interactions between material points. The state-based formulation of this effective energy-based criterion is as follows.

(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}\in{\cal H}_{\bf x}\subset v_{i})

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\langle\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}^{\top}\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{K}$}}\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}\rangle_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}^{\top}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{K}$}}\mbox{$\langle$}\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}\rangle_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$})+\Delta^{\rm UKU}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}),

(6.6)

\displaystyle\Delta^{\rm UKU}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int[\mbox{$\mathbb{A}$}_{ij}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$})\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}]^{\top}\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{K}$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$})[\mbox{$\mathbb{A}$}_{ij}(\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$})\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}]\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}\mid;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})~d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j},

(6.7)

where the statistical average of the displacement state is defined as

\mbox{$\langle$}\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}\rangle_{i}[\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}]\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\mbox{$\rangle$}=\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf A$}}^{u\epsilon*}_{i}[\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}]\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\mbox{$\rangle$}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf A$}^{\eta\epsilon*}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\mbox{$\rangle$}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}
(
4.17
);
\mathbb{K}
is the modulus state (
2.13
)
[
420
]
.
The energy-based failure model
[
237
]
evaluates the local strain energy density in bond-based peridynamics. From this, an effective criterion for the state-based model can be derived

\displaystyle\!\!\!4\mbox{$\langle$}{\cal W}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle\!\!=\!\!

\displaystyle\langle\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}^{\top}\bullet\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{K}$}}\bullet\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})=\langle\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}^{\top}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}\bullet\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{K}$}}\bullet\mbox{$\langle$}\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})+\Delta^{\rm UKU}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),

(6.8)

\displaystyle\Delta^{\rm UKU}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\int\!\!\Big\langle[\mbox{$\mathbb{A}$}_{ij}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$})\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}]^{\top}\big\langle\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{K}$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$})[\mbox{$\mathbb{A}$}_{ij}(\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$})\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}]\big\rangle^{\cal H_{\bf x}}\Big\rangle^{\cal H_{\bf x}}\!\!(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

(6.9)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}\mid;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})~d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j}

averaging over the horizon region,
{\cal H}_{\bf x}\subset v_{i}

(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}\in v_{j}
).

Seemingly, a second moment of the force vector state
\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf T$}}=\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{K}$}}\bullet\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}
(
2.19
) can be obtained

(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}\in{\cal H}_{\bf x}\subset v_{i}
)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mbox{$\langle$}\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf T$}}\otimes\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf T$}}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}^{\cal H_{\bf x}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\langle(\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{K}$}}\bullet\mbox{$\langle$}\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i})\otimes(\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{K}$}}\bullet\mbox{$\langle$}\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i})\Big\rangle^{\cal H_{\bf x}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\Delta^{\rm T2}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),

(6.10)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\Delta^{\rm T2}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\!\!\Big\langle\big\langle\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{K}$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$})[\mbox{$\mathbb{A}$}_{ij}(\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$})\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}]\big\rangle^{\cal H_{\bf x}}\otimes\big\langle\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{K}$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$})

(6.11)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle[\mbox{$\mathbb{A}$}_{ij}(\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$})\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}]\big\rangle^{\cal H_{\bf x}}\Big\rangle^{\cal H_{\bf x}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}\mid;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})~d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j}.

From Eq. (
6.10
), we can determine
\mbox{$\langle$}\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf T$}}\cdot\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf T$}}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}^{\cal H_{\bf x}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in{\cal H}_{\bf x}\subset v_{i}
), providing an estimate for the yield function. Specifically, the criterion based on the co-deviatoric force state is given by:

\psi(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\big\langle\underline{t}^{\rm d}[x]\mbox{$\langle$}\xi\mbox{$\rangle$}

\times\underline{t}^{\rm d}[x]\mbox{$\langle$}\xi\mbox{$\rangle$}\rangle^{\cal H_{\bf x}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})/2
.
This corresponds to the second invariant of the deviatoric stress tensor in classical local theory, where
\underline{t}^{\rm d}[x]\mbox{$\langle$}\xi\mbox{$\rangle$}
represents the co-deviatoric component of the scalar force state
\underline{t}
(see
[
349
]
,
[
420
]
for details).

A failure criterion for bond breakage between material points is based on energy dissipation,
{\cal W}_{\bf\xi}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(see
[
177
]
,
[
433
]
,
[
434
]
,
[
456
]
). It depends on the stress state
\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf T$}}=\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{K}$}}\bullet\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}
(
2.19
) and the displacement state
\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}
between two points (
\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\in{\cal H}_{\bf x}\subset v_{i})

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\cal W}_{\bf\xi}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\langle(\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{K}$}}\bullet\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}})\rangle_{i}\langle\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}\big\rangle_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=(\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{K}$}}^{(i)}\bullet\langle\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}\rangle_{i})\langle\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}\rangle_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\Delta^{\rm TU2}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),

(6.12)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\Delta^{\rm TU2}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=\!\!

\displaystyle\int\!\!\big\langle\underline{\mbox{$\mathbb{K}$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$})[\mbox{$\mathbb{A}$}_{ij}(\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$})\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}]\big\rangle^{\cal H_{\bf x}}[\mbox{$\mathbb{A}$}_{ij}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$})\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}](\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\varphi(v_{j},\!{\bf x}_{j}\!\mid;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j}.

(6.13)

Equations (
6.6
)-(
6.13
) for the linear operators
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal A$}^{\theta\theta}_{i}
(
3.2
2
) and
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{p}^{\theta\zeta}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})
(
3.2
1
) simplify to the corresponding equations for both the linear state-based
[
86
]
and bond-based
[
90
]
models.

Comment 6.2.

All effective energy-based criteria in (
6.6
)-(
6.13
) are derived from a single fundamental component: the second moment of relative displacement
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\otimes\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})
(
6.3
) (or, that is the same,
\mbox{$\langle$}\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}[\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}]\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\mbox{$\rangle$}\otimes\underline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}[\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}]\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\mbox{$\rangle$}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}
,
\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\in{\cal H}_{\bf x}\subset v_{i}
)).
These criteria are defined on
either one bond
\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}\in{\cal H}_{\bf x}\subset v_{i}
, or two bonds
\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}\in{\cal H}_{\bf x}\subset v_{i}
with the field fluctuations, either

\Delta^{\rm UKU}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}),\Delta^{\rm UKU}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
,
\Delta^{\rm T2}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
, or

\Delta^{\rm TU2}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
. All the field fluctuations can be incorporated into the dataset
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
(
4.2
).

Comment 6.3.

Effective energy-based criteria using field second moments were developed for the linear bond-based
[
86
]
and state-based
[
90
]
models, while Eq. (
6.3
) applies to nonlinear constitutive laws
[
94
]
. A promising approach would be to estimate effective strain energy directly, as in
[
378
]
, without preliminary computing field second moments.

7
CMs with other constitutive laws of phases

7.1
Locally elastic

We will consider the local basic equations of thermoelastostatics
(
2.1
)-(
2.3
) of composites. Locally elastic counterpart of Eqs. (
3.17
) and (
4.6
) are

{}^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})-^{L}\!\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=^{L}\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\zeta\zeta}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},^{L}\!\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}),\ \ ^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=^{L}\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal A$}^{\zeta\zeta}(^{L}\!\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),

(7.1)

where we introduce the substitutions
(\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$},\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$})\leftrightarrow\,^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}
.
The most straightforward method is reduced to the solution of the integral Eq. (
3.12
) using the Green function
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf G$}^{(0)}

and replacing
\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}^{w\Gamma}\rightarrow\,^{L}\!\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
. For a homogeneous ellipsoidal inclusion under a uniform effective field, this yields Eshelby’s solution
[
165
]
. When the effective field is inhomogeneous, the multipole expansion method
[
280
]
is effective.
For an infinite medium, the volume integral equation (VIE)
[
80
]
,
[
293
]
applies, allowing discretization of inclusions only, unlike FEA (which is effectively used at
{}^{L}\!\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\equiv
const.). The boundary integral equation (BIE) methods
[
25
]
,
[
221
]
,
[
354
]
,
[
432
]
are widely used for homogeneous elasticity but face challenges like 3D meshing and singular integral computation. Alternative meshless methods
[
172
]
, such as local boundary integral equation, boundary knot
method, boundary collocation method, non-dimensional dynamic influence functions
method, and the method of fundamental solutions (MFS)
[
281
]
(see also
[
107
]
), offer advantages. In Amic (
3.2
), MFS
[
70
]
adapted to CAM is most effective. Each method has its strengths and limitations, requiring careful selection based on application.

We also consider a case of an imperfect interface

\displaystyle[[\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})]]\not={\bf 0},\ \ [[\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})]]\cdot\mbox{\boldmath$\bf n$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\not={\bf 0},\

(7.2)

with the jumps (
7.2
1
) and (
7.2
2
) at the

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in\Gamma_{i}^{u}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in\Gamma_{i}^{\sigma}

(
\Gamma_{i}^{u},\Gamma_{i}^{\sigma}\subset\Gamma_{i}
), respectively, described by the different models of interface imperfections.
As such, in the right-hand side of Eq. (
3.12
) with the volume integral, we need to add the surface integrals
with some Green functions kernels and integrands (
7.2
1
) and (
7.2
2
) (see for details
[
70
]
).
The first kind of model can be
