referred to as interface models in which the traction is continuous across the interface
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in\Gamma_{i}^{u}
while the displacement is in general discontinuities (
7.2
1
) at
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in\Gamma_{i}^{u}
such as, e.g., in
linear spring model
(LSM, see, e.g.,

[
212
]
,
[
213
]
,
[
214
]
,
[
144
]
,
[
230
]
,
[
510
]
). The LSM was generalized to a
cohesive zone model
(CZM),
where the traction vector assumed to be continuous is a non-linear
function of the displacement jump (bilinear, trapezoidal, exponential, and polynomial
cohesive laws). The cohesive model originated by Barenblatt
[
26
]
in fracture mechanics (see also

[
357
]
,
[
365
]
) has received wide development in micromechanics of CM (see, e.g.,

[
368
]
,
[
436
]
,
[
437
]
).

The second kind of interface model, the Gurtin–Murdoch interface stress model (ISM)
[
202
]
,
[
203
]

(see also
[
233
]
,

[
334
]
,
[
379
]
,
[
451
]
), also known as the coherent interface model, is dual to the linear spring-layer model. It enforces displacement continuity across
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf s$}\in\Gamma_{i}^{\sigma}\subset\Gamma_{i}
while allowing discontinuities in stress and strain. Steigmann and Ogden
[
431
]
extended ISM to account for surface resistance to stretching and bending by incorporating surface membrane strain and curvature tensors. ISM has been applied to nanoscale structures
[
346
]
,
[
411
]
and adapted for Eshelby-type inclusion problems
[
109
]
,
[
110
]
,
[
151
]
,
[
216
]
,
[
409
]
,
[
410
]
,
see also
[
180
]
,
[
181
]
. All imperfect interface models can be incorporated into the perturbator
{}^{L}\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\zeta\zeta}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},^{L}\!\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})
(
7.1
)
[
70
]
.

Replacing the PM perturbator (
3.17
) with the LM perturbator (
7.1
) transforms AGIE (
3.23
) into the LM-based AGIE:

\displaystyle\langle^{L}\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}^{b(0)}({\bf x})+\int{{}^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}^{\zeta\zeta}_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j},^{L}\!\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}|v_{1},{\bf x}_{1})d{\bf x}_{j}

(7.3)

where
\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}^{b(0)}({\bf x})
represents the deterministic field induced by the body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
in an infinite homogeneous matrix.
When the perturbator
{}^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\zeta\zeta}_{j}
is expressed via the Green function (as in Eq. (
3.12
)), the left-hand side and integral terms in Eqs. (
3.12
) and (
7.3
) match. However, Eq. (
7.3
)—derived for body forces (
2.4
)—is exact, whereas Eq. (
3.12
), formulated for remote boundary conditions (
2.30
), is incorrect.

Similarly to Eq. (
7.3
), the second field moment within an inclusion
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}
is given by

\displaystyle\!\!\langle^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}\otimes^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})\otimes\langle^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})+\!\!\int\!\!^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal A$}^{\zeta\zeta}_{i}*\mbox{$\langle$}^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{p}^{\zeta\zeta}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},^{L}\!\overline{\zeta})|;v_{i},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i}\mbox{$\rangle$}

(7.4)

\displaystyle\otimes

{}^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal A$}^{\theta\theta}_{i}*\mbox{$\langle$}^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{p}^{\zeta\zeta}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},^{L}\!\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})|;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i}\rangle\varphi(v_{p},{\bf x}_{p}\mid;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})d{\bf x}_{p}.

As expected, this equation coincides with its counterpart in LM under remote boundary conditions (
2.30
), as derived in
[
80
]
.

Solution of Eq. (
7.3
) is performed by repeating step-by-step of solution of Eq. (
3.23
), see Eqs. (
4.5
)-(
4.2
). Finally, we obtain a dataset
(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in R^{d})

\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{}^{L}\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm r}

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\{^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N},

\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{}^{L}\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm r}_{k}

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\{\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\!\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\!\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}_{ik}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\!\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}}_{ik}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\},

(7.5)

which looks as the dataset
{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm r}
(
4.2
). Analysis of periodic structure CM is reduced to replacement of probability densities
\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})
and
\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i}|;v_{j},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j})
by their
\delta
function representations
(
2.36
) that leads to a dataset
{}^{L}\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm p}
similar to
{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm p}
(
5
).

7.2
Strongly nonlocal (strain type) model

Following Rogula
[
389
]
and Eringen
[
164
]
, the stress-strain relationship in nonlocal linear thermoelasticity is governed by the integral equation:

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}*\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal M$}*\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

(7.6)

where the integral response operators
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal K$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$},\mbox{\boldmath$\cal M$};\ \mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$},\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}
) and transformation field
\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$},\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}

are defined as

\mbox{\boldmath$\cal K$}*(\cdot)=\int\mbox{\boldmath$\cal K$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})(\cdot)(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$},\ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\int\mbox{\boldmath$\bf m$}^{\gamma}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})\Delta T(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}.

(7.7)

Here,
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal K$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})
is the kernel governing nonlocal interactions, while
\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
represents the transformation field, assuming a uniform temperature difference
\Delta T(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})\equiv(T-T^{0})
. This formulation modifies only the stress-strain relation, keeping equilibrium and compatibility equations unchanged, as in linear isotropic elasticity
[
31
]
,
[
154
]
,
[
163
]
,
[
164
]
,
[
266
]
.

The response and inverse response parameters in Eq. (
7.6
) satisfy the relationships:

\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}\!*\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal M$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal M$}\!*\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf I$},\ \ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\cal M$}\!*\!\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}=-\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$},\ \ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}\!*\!\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}=-\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$},

(7.8)

with material parameters subjected to the following symmetry regulations:

\mbox{\boldmath$\cal K$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$},\mbox{\boldmath$\cal M$};\ \mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$},\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}
):

{\cal K}_{ijkl}={\cal K}_{ijlk}={\cal K}_{jikl}
,
{\cal K}_{ijkl}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})={\cal K}_{klij}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
,
\gamma_{ij}=\gamma_{ji}

[
276
]
,
[
268
]
.
The kernel
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal K$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})
is given by:

\mbox{\boldmath$\cal K$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf K$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\lambda(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}),

(7.9)

where
\lambda(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})
is a nonlocal weight function, often distance-dependent
\lambda(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})=\lambda_{\infty}(|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}|)

[
376
]
, and normalized as:

\int_{w}\lambda(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}=1,\ \ \forall\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in w=R^{d}

(7.10)

This ensures
\cal K
represents stiffness or compliance in uniform straining and stressing. In the limit
\lambda_{\infty}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\to\delta(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
, the nonlocal operator reduces to the local case
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal K$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})\to\mbox{\boldmath$\bf K$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\delta(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})
, recovering the classical formulation (
2.2
).

Micromechanical models begin with analyzing a single heterogeneity in an infinite matrix under remote loading
[
274
]
(see comprehensive reviews in
[
59
]
,
[
201
]
, and
[
334
]
).
For various nonlocal models, involving spatial integrals or field gradients, the internal field within a homogeneous ellipsoidal inclusion remains non-uniform even under homogeneous remote loading. The strongly nonlocal strain-based counterparts of Eqs. (
3.17
) and (
4.6
) are:

{}^{S}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})-^{S}\!\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=^{S}\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\zeta\zeta}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},^{S}\!\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}),\ \ ^{S}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=^{S}\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal A$}^{\zeta\zeta}(^{S}\!\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),

(7.11)

where
(\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$},\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$})\leftrightarrow\,^{S}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}
. Unlike MEF and MTM (see Comment 3.4), which focus on perturbators inside the inclusion
[see e.g.
[
408
]
,
[
477
]
,
[
505
]
], CAM requires evaluating
{}^{S}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\zeta\zeta}_{i}

outside
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\not\in v_{i}
(see
[
96
]
). This was addressed in
[
59
]
using an iterative solution of the volume integral equation.

Substituting the PM perturbator (
3.17
) with the strongly nonlocal strain-based perturbator (
7.11
) modifies AGIE (
3.23
) into the following nonlocal AGIE formulation:

\displaystyle\langle^{S}\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}^{b(0)}({\bf x})+\int{{}^{S}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}^{\zeta\zeta}_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j},^{S}\!\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}|v_{1},{\bf x}_{1})d{\bf x}_{j}

(7.12)

where
\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}^{b(0)}({\bf x})
denotes the deterministic field generated by the body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
in an infinite homogeneous matrix.
By centering Eq. (
7.12
), we obtain a GIE analogous to (
3.24
), which was applied in
[
59
]
to study CMs reinforced with circular inclusions. It was shown that the local statistical average stress
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}
) depends on both the radial distribution functions (RDFs) and the excluded volume
v_{i}^{0}
, potentially leading to stress values that differ in sign from those predicted by classical methods such as the MEF and MTM. However, the differences in the effective moduli
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{*}
estimated using GIEs—nonlocal counterparts to Eqs. (
3.13
) and (
3.14
)—were found to be relatively minor.

To estimate the second field moment
\langle^{S}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}\otimes^{S}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})
within an inclusion
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}
, one simply needs to replace the superscript
L
with
S
in Eq. (
7.3
). Likewise, the dataset
{}^{D}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}
can be derived from Eq. (
7.1
) by substituting the superscript
L
with
S
.

7.3
Coupled problems of composites

We formulate the Additive GIEs for coupled problems using thermoelectroelasticity as an example. To maintain consistency, elastic and electric variables are treated equivalently. Following the notation from
[
28
]
and referencing works like
[
336
]
,
[
373
]
, and
[
251
]
, the local linear constitutive relations are recast in a unified form, leading to a basic equation analogous to Eqs. (
2.1
)–(
2.3
).

\cal D
\Sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\bf 0$},\ \mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}\mbox{\boldmath$\cal U$},

(7.13)

\cal E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{$\mathbb{M}$}\mbox{\boldmath$\Sigma$}+\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$},\ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\Sigma$}=\mbox{$\mathbb{L}$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}),

(7.14)

where

\cal E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\\
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf E$}\end{array}\right\|,\ \mbox{\boldmath$\Sigma$}=\left\|\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\\
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf D$}\end{array}\right\|,\ \mbox{\boldmath$\cal U$}=\left\|\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\\
\phi\end{array}\right\|,\ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}=\left\|\begin{array}[]{cc}{\rm def}\ \ \ \ 0\\
0\ \ {\rm grad}\end{array}\right\|,

\mathbb{M}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\begin{array}[]{cc}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf M$}\ \ \ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf d$}^{\top}\\
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf d$}\ \ -\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}\end{array}\right\|,\ \ \mbox{$\mathbb{L}$}=\left\|\begin{array}[]{cc}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}\ \ \ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}^{\top}\\
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}\ \ -\mbox{\boldmath$\bf k$}\end{array}\right\|,\ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}=\left\|\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{\boldmath$\beta$}\\
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf q$}\theta\end{array}\right\|,\ \

here
\bf D
and
\bf E
are the vectors of induction and
electric field intensity,
\theta
is a deviation of
a stationary temperature field from a given value,

\bf k
and
\bf b
are the tensors of dielectric permeability and impermeability,

\bf q
is the pyroelectric coefficient,

\bf e
and
\bf d
are the piezoelectric moduli, and
\phi
is the electric potential.
To obtain a symmetric matrix of coefficients we replaced the electric
field
\bf E
by
-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf E$}
, and the tensors
\bf k
and
\bf b
by
-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf k$}
and
-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}

on the right-hand sides of (
7.14
). It is assumed that the properties of both the comparison medium and the
matrix coincide. Some background representations for micromechanics of thermoelectroelasticity such as generalized Hill’s conditions, effective energy functions, and phase-averaged first and second moments are considered in
[
54
]
(see also
[
381
]
).

These and other methods with the classical background defined by both the hypothesis
H1
and GIEs counterpart of (
3.13
) (see, e.g. Dinzart and Sabar, 2011; Levin
et al.
, 2011; Lu
et al.
, 2011; and referenced in Buryachenko, 2013b)
are based on the estimation of the average field and polarization tensors inside the heterogeneities rather than outside ones.
Exploiting of the new background (
3.25
) and (
3.26
) is accomplished by a straightforward generalization of the approaches of Subsections 4.1 and 4.2 taking
the unified notations (
7.13
)-(
7.3
) into account.

The coupled electroelasticity counterparts of Eqs. (
3.17
) and (
4.6
) are given by
(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in R^{d}
):

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})-\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=^{EE}\!\!\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}^{EE}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$}}),\ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=^{EE}\!\!\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal A$}}^{EE}(\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$}})(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),

(7.31)

Unlike classical methods such as MEF and MTM, which rely on hypothesis
H1a
(
3.8
) and the GIE counterpart of (
3.13
) (see
[
131
]
,
[
132
]
,
[
149
]
,
[
150
]
,
[
152
]
,
[
200
]
,
[
301
]
,

[
302
]
,
[
303
]
,
[
256
]
,
[
413
]
), the CAM approach (both Eqs. (
3.23
) and (
3.24
)) requires evaluating
{}^{EE}\!\!\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}}^{EE}_{i}
(
7.31
) outside the inclusion region (
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\not\in v_{i}
), rather than just inside (
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}
). For inclusions of arbitrary shape and non-uniform effective fields (
\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\not\equiv
const.), numerical methods mentioned in Subsections
4.1 and 4.2 must also be adapted accordingly.

Replacing the PM perturbator (
3.17
) with the coupled electroelasticity perturbator (
7.31
) transforms AGIE (
3.23
) into:

\displaystyle\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$}^{b(0)}({\bf x})+\int{{}^{EE}}\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{EE}_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$}})\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}|v_{1},{\bf x}_{1})d{\bf x}_{j}.

(7.32)

Here,
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$}^{b(0)}({\bf x})
represents the deterministic field produced by the coupled body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
in an infinite homogeneous matrix.
By centering Eq. (
7.32
), we derive a GIE analogous to (
3.24
), which was introduced in
[
80
]
for analyzing random-structured piezoelectric composite materials.

To estimate the second field moment
\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$}\otimes\mbox{\boldmath$\cal E$}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})
within an inclusion
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}
, one only needs to replace the superscript
L
with
EE
in Eq. (
7.3
). Similarly, the dataset
{}^{EE}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
can be obtained in the same way as
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
(
7.1
).

7.4
First Strain Gradient Medium

Subsections 6.3 and 6.4 provide an excerpt from
[
80
]
.

In the linearized theory of Mindlin’s form-II first strain gradient elasticity (
[
347
]
,
[
348
]
, see also
[
289
]
,
[
375
]
,
[
429
]
), the strain energy density of a homogeneous centrosymmetric material depends quadratically on both the strain
\varepsilon
and its gradient
\mbox{\boldmath$\kappa$}=\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}
:

W(\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$},\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$})={1\over 2}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}:\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}:\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}+{1\over 2}\mbox{\boldmath$\kappa$}\therefore\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}\therefore\mbox{\boldmath$\kappa$}={1\over 2}L_{ijkl}\varepsilon_{ij}\varepsilon_{kl}+{1\over 2}C_{ijmkln}\partial_{m}\varepsilon_{ij}\partial_{n}\varepsilon_{kl},

(7.33)

where
\bf L
is the rank-four elasticity tensor, and
\bf C
is the rank-six strain gradient elasticity tensor, both possessing specific symmetries (
[
80
]
). The operator
\therefore
denotes contraction over the last three indices of the left operand and the first three of the right.

Using the Euler–Lagrange equations, the corresponding static governing equation for displacement takes the Navier-like form:

\mbox{$\mathbb{L}$}^{\rm M}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}=-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},\ \ {\mbox{$\mathbb{L}$}}^{\rm M}={\mbox{$\mathbb{L}$}}-{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}},\ \ {\mbox{$\mathbb{L}$}}_{ik}=L_{ijkl}\partial_{j}\partial_{l},\ \ {\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}_{ik}=C_{ijmklm}\partial_{j}\partial_{l}\partial_{m}\partial_{n}.

(7.34)

Thus, the Mindlin operator
\mbox{$\mathbb{L}$}^{\rm M}
incorporates both standard elasticity and strain gradient effects, ensuring a symmetric total stress tensor in the first strain-gradient model
[
21
]
.
Some well-known simplified strain gradient theories, such as couple stress theory,
Aifantis, Kleinert, Mindlin, and Wei & Hutchinson’s models (
[
7
]
,
[
13
]
,
[
21
]
,
[
262
]
,
[
285
]
,

[
347
]
,
[
512
]
) have similar forms of governing equations as that of the general isotropic second gradient theory (see their connection in
[
80
]
).

Consider an inhomogeneity
v_{i}
centered at
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i}
with material parameters
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
7.32
) embedded in an infinite homogeneous matrix
w
characterized by
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{(0)}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{(0)}
. Under generalized traction-free remote boundary conditions and body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
2.4
), the resulting fields in the homogeneous matrix (without
v_{i}
) are
\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}^{b}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
and
\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\kappa$}}^{b}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in w
). The equilibrium equation takes the form:

\mbox{$\mathbb{L}$}^{\rm M(0)}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}=-\mbox{$\mathbb{L}$}^{\rm M}_{1}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$},

(7.35)

where
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}):=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{(0)}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}):=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{(0)}
vanish outside
v_{i}
.

Following
[
427
]
, we define the stress and double-stress polarization tensors:

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{(0)}:\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\ \ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\mu$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{(0)}\therefore\mbox{\boldmath$\kappa$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),

(7.36)

which are zero outside
v_{i}
. Substituting these into the equilibrium equation (
7.35
) results in:

\mbox{$\mathbb{L}$}^{\rm M(0)}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}=-\nabla[\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}-\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}].

(7.37)

For convenience, we introduce the notation:

\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\\
\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}\end{pmatrix},\ \ \ \hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\\
\mbox{\boldmath$\kappa$}\end{pmatrix},\ \ \ \hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}}=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}\\
\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\kappa$}}\end{pmatrix},\ \ \ \hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}^{\epsilon\tau}&\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}^{\epsilon\pi}\\
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}^{\kappa\tau}&\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}^{\kappa\pi}\end{pmatrix},

(7.38)

Here,
\hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}}=(\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\kappa$}})
represents the field in a homogeneous medium with
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{(0)}
,
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{(0)}
under given BC (like (
2.4
)). The Green functions and their derivatives,
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}^{\epsilon\tau}=\nabla\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\bf G$}^{\rm M},

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}^{\epsilon\pi}=-\nabla\nabla\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\bf G$}^{\rm M}
,

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}^{\kappa\tau}=\nabla\nabla\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\bf G$}^{\rm M}
,
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}^{\kappa\tau}=

-\nabla\nabla\nabla\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\bf G$}^{\rm M}
, relevant for both isotropic and anisotropic media, are detailed in
[
290
]
and
[
375
]
.

By applying the Green function (
7.38
) and integrating by parts, the equilibrium equation (
7.38
) is transformed into the convolution form

\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}=\hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}}+\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}*\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}},

(7.39)

which corresponds to Eq. (
3.11
) up to notation differences.

The integral equation (
7.39
) with a nonsingular convolution kernel
\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}
can be rewritten in operator form as

\displaystyle\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})-\hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle:=

{}^{\epsilon}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}})=^{\epsilon}\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},\hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}}),

\displaystyle\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

{}^{\epsilon}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal A$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},\hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}}),

(7.40)

Particular case of homogeneous effective field
\hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\equiv
const. and homogeneous ellipsoidal inclusion corresponds to the Eshelby solution considered in
[
187
]
,
[
322
]
,
[
505
]
,
[
508
]
.
In the general case of the inclusion shape and inhomogeneity of the effective field
\hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\not\equiv
const.,
the solutions
{}^{\epsilon}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}})
and

{}^{\epsilon}\!bfcA_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},\hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}})
can be obtained via the volume integral equation method (Subsection 6.1) or finite element analysis (e.g.,
[
21
]
,
[
138
]
,
[
448
]
).

Replacing the PM perturbator (
3.17
) with the strain gradient perturbator (
7.4
) transforms AGIE (
3.23
) into:

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}^{b(0)}({\bf x})+\int{{}^{\epsilon}}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{j}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j},\hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}})\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}|v_{1},{\bf x}_{1})d{\bf x}_{j}.

(7.41)

Here,
\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}^{b(0)}({\bf x})
represents the deterministic field produced by the body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
in an infinite homogeneous matrix.
By centering Eq. (
7.41
), we derive a GIE analogous to (
3.24
), which was introduced in
[
80
]
for analyzing random-structured CMs with strain gradient properties of phases.

To estimate the second field moment
\langle\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}\otimes\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}}\rangle_{i}({\bf x})
within an inclusion
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in v_{i}
, one only needs to replace the superscript
L
with
ϵ
in Eq. (
7.4
). Similarly, the dataset
{}^{\epsilon}\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
can be obtained in the same way as
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
(
7.1
).

7.5
Stress-Gradient Elasticity Model

In this subsection, we briefly summarize the stress-gradient elasticity framework, introduced by Forest and Sab
[
176
]
and further developed in
[
49
]
,
[
390
]
,
[
445
]
, adapted for obtaining AGIE (as discussed in Section 3 for PM). In the strain-gradient model (
7.33
), the strain-energy density
W
depends on the strain
\varepsilon
and its gradient
\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}
. Similarly, in the stress-gradient model of Forest and Sab
[
176
]
(see also
[
80
]
), the complementary strain-energy density

W^{c}

of a linear homogeneous centrosymmetric material
depends on the stress
\sigma

and its first gradient
\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}

W^{c}(\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf R$})={1\over 2}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}:\mbox{\boldmath$\bf M$}:\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}+{1\over 2}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf R$}\therefore\mbox{\boldmath$\bf S$}\therefore\mbox{\boldmath$\bf R$},

(7.42)

where
\bf M
and
\bf S
are the classical and generalized compliances which are tensors with minor
and major symmetries. The trace-free part
\bf R
of the stress-gradient
\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}
is the orthogonal projection of
\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}

onto the space of third-rank, trace-free tensors, and we write
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf R$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf I$}^{\prime}_{6}\therefore\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}
, where the sixth-rank
tensor
I^{\prime}_{6|ijkpqr}=I_{ijpq}\delta_{kr}-(I_{pq(ir}\delta_{j)k})/2

[
445
]
is defined as the orthogonal projection (in the sense of the
\therefore
scalar product).
Being a projector,
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf I$}^{\prime}_{p}
enjoys the classical property
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf I$}^{\prime}_{p}\therefore\mbox{\boldmath$\bf I$}^{\prime}_{p}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf I$}^{\prime}_{p}
.

The strain measures, the energy-conjugate to the stress
\sigma
and trace-free variable
\bf R
, are

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf M$}:\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}={\partial W^{c}\over\partial\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}},\ \ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\phi$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf S$}\therefore\mbox{\boldmath$\bf R$}={\partial W^{c}\over\partial\mbox{\boldmath$\bf R$}},

(7.43)

where the total strain
\bf e
is not necessarily the symmetric gradient of the displacement
\bf u
.

Equations (
7.42
) can be considered as an inversion of Eq. (
7.43
)

\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}:\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$},\ \ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf R$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}\therefore\mbox{\boldmath$\phi$},

(7.44)

where
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}=(\bf M)^{-1}
is a classical stiffness whereas the generalized compliance
\bf S
and stiffness
\bf C
are inverse to each other

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}\therefore\mbox{\boldmath$\bf S$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf S$}\therefore\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf I$}^{\prime}_{6}
.

Analogously to (
7.36
), we introduce the stress and stress gradient polarization tensors

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{(0)}:\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\ \ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf R$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{(0)}\therefore\mbox{\boldmath$\phi$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),

(7.45)

vanishing in the matrix
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in w\setminus v_{i}
. Similarly to Eq. (
7.39
) (see also
[

]
), we get an equation for one inhomogeneity in the infinite matrix

\displaystyle\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}}=\hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}}}+^{\bf e}\!\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}*\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}},

(7.46)

where

\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}\\
\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}\end{pmatrix},\ \ \ \hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}\\
\mbox{\boldmath$\phi$}\end{pmatrix},\ \ \ \hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}}}=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}}\\
\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\phi$}}\end{pmatrix},\ \ \ ^{\bf e}\!\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}=\begin{pmatrix}{}^{\bf e}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}^{e\tau}&{}^{\bf e}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}^{e\pi}\\
{}^{\bf e}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}^{\phi\tau}&{}^{\bf e}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}^{\phi\pi}\end{pmatrix},

(7.47)

where
\hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}}}=(\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\pi$}})
is a field that would exist in the medium with homogeneous properties
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf M$}^{(0)}
,
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf S$}^{(0)}
and
appropriate remote boundary condition. Green operators
{}^{\bf e}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}^{e\pi}
,

{}^{\bf e}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}^{\phi\tau}
, and
{}^{\bf e}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}^{\phi\pi}
can be derived from the single operator
{}^{\bf e}\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}^{e\tau}
,
as obtained by Tran
[

]
for an isotropic simplified model.

The integral Eq. (
7.46
) with nonsingular convolution kernel
\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf U$}}
and their solution can be presented in an operator form
(

) and (

)

\displaystyle\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})-\hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle:=

{}^{\bf e}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\tau}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}})=^{\bf e}\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{e}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},\hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}}}),

\displaystyle\bar{v}_{i}\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\tau$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

{}^{\bf e}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal R$}^{e}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},\hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}}}),

(7.48)

where the solutions
{}^{\bf e}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{e}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},\hat{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}})
and
{}^{\bf e}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal R$}^{e}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},\hat{\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf e$}}})

by finite element analysis (see, e.g.,

8
Representative volume element (EVE)

8.1
RVE for CMs subjected to remote homogeneous loading

The Representative Volume Element (RVE), introduced by Hill
[
217
]
, has a complex history (see
[
366
]
). To accurately convey its definition and significance, we cite Hill
[
217
]
, which provides its rigorous foundation.

Definition 8.1.

Representative volume element (RVE)
(a) is structurally entirely typical of the whole mixture on average, and
(b) contains a sufficient number of inclusions for the apparent overall moduli (
and statistically averaged field distributions in the phases

) to
be effectively independent of the surface values of traction and displacement, so
long as these values are ‘macroscopically uniform…. The contribution of this surface layer to any average can be negligible by taking the sample large enough.

In Hill’s original definition
[
217
]
, a
small caps
font fragment was included and is explained later. We highlight key RVE concepts reflecting this definition:

1. The RVE applies to statistically homogeneous or periodic CMs under remote homogeneous boundary conditions (BCs) (
2.28
) or (
2.29
). While Hill
[
217
]
did not use terms like ”statistically homogeneous” or ”periodic structure CMs,” the concept aligns with them. Additionally, ”functionally graded structures” (a later term) do not fit within the RVE framework.

2. The RVE estimates effective moduli
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{*}
by averaging phase fields. For periodic structure, CMs, RVE is equivalent to a unit cell (RVE
\equiv
UC), and remote homogeneous BCs (
2.28
) or (
2.29
) reduce to periodic BCs (
2.39
). For statistically homogeneous CMs, no specific geometric representation is required. However, in practice, RVE sampling via computational or image-based methods is common, and verifying finite-size BC replacements is crucial to avoid distortions in property estimation.

3. Hill
[
217
]
applied his definition to linear locally elastic CMs, but Definition 8.1 omits the term “constitutive law.” It generalizes to peridynamic CMs by replacing “surface” (
2.42
), (
2.43
) with “volumetric” (
2.40
), (
2.41
).

The RVE concept, widely used in practice, often serves as a heuristic rather than a strict mathematical definition. Engineers rely on intuitive choices based on experience, especially for complex microstructures unsuitable for analytical or computational homogenization. However, such approximations can be misleading when microstructural details significantly impact material behavior. In practice, RVEs for finite samples (with BC or VBC) are generated using two main approaches:
(1) random inclusion fields (synonymous with “arbitrary simulated fields”, e.g.,
[
362
]
,
[
463
]
,
[
481
]
,
[
482
]
) and (2) image-based methods like micro-CT for modeling “real” RVEs (e.g.,
[
5
]
,
[
15
]
,
[
435
]
).

The inaccuracies of intuitive RVEs in PM have long been addressed in LM (see the Introduction for references, see also

[
188
]
,
[
211
]
,
[
252
]
,
[
353
]
,
[
393
]
,
[
439
]
). In LM, a sample response is estimated from DNS of microstructural volume elements (MVEs) simulated or extracted (e.g., from micro-CT). The homogenized properties
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{\rm A}_{\rm KUBC}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf M$}^{\rm A}_{\rm SUBC}
for KUBC (
2.41
) and SUBC (
2.42
) are different. The convergence of
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{\rm A}_{\rm KUBC}-(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf M$}^{\rm A}_{\rm SUBC})^{-1}
as MVE size increases help approximate the RVE size and estimate the effective moduli
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{*}
, requiring large (theoretically infinite) material domains (see
[
366
]
and
[
80
]
). However, even with convergence in the number of realizations (e.g.,
[
362
]
), boundary layer and edge scale effects may persist. The approaches used in LM for RVE investigations are based on Block 3 methods (
3.1
), which are well-developed for peridynamics (see Subsequent 3.4), making their use in PM straightforward.

Another well-known inconsistency in LM is the replacement of PBC by homogeneous BC (see
[
54
]
, p. 508). The same issues are expected in PM when modeling infinite (e.g., periodic) media with finite domains:
(see, e.g.,

[
5
]
,

[
17
]
,

[
18
]
,

[
19
]
,

[
113
]
,

[
120
]

[
242
]
,

[
483
]
,

[
499
]
,

[
506
]
). Using body force with compact support (e.g., (
4.5
)) as a training parameter helps eliminate sample size, boundary layer, and edge effects.

In the context of AMic, the RVE interpretation for random structure CMs avoids issues like sample size and edge effects, as well as the need for “surface values.” This is because the domain is the full space
R^{d}
, and homogeneous BCs or PBCs are not a concern. The RVE size is now fully determined by the micromechanical model used, making the concept of “taking the sample large enough” irrelevant.
For both random and periodic structure CMs, the effective moduli
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{*}
and field concentration factors
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf A$}^{*}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})
(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}\in v_{i}
) are related by mutual coupling (see
small caps
in Definition 8.1), see equation

^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{*}=^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{(0)}+^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf R$}^{*},\ \ \ \mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})=^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf A$}^{*}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$})\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}.

(8.1)

In random structure CMs, the RVE size depends on the micromechanical estimation method. For one-particle methods (like EFM, MTM, and EMM, see Comment 3.4), the RVE is defined as
v_{i}
, and the interaction size
a^{\rm int}=a
. In the multiparticle EFM, considering binary interactions increases
a^{\rm int}=6a
, thus increasing the RVE size.
In PM, methods for one-particle approaches yield different
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{*}
estimations with the same RVE size. This eliminates the need for sample size control in Amic methods. For periodic structure CMs, the RVE coincides with the unit cell (UC). Therefore, under the hypothesis (
2.33
2
) in LM, the equality
\rm RVE^{\bf L^{*}}\equiv RVE^{\mbox{$\langle$}\bf\epsilon\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}({\bf z})}
holds for both random and periodic structure CMs.

8.2
RVE for CMs subjected to inhomogeneous loading

The RVE concept is rigorously valid when the field scale (inhomogeneity) vastly exceeds the material scale (microstructural variations, see (
2.33
2
), (
2.34
2
). When these scales are comparable, nonlocal material behavior emerges
requiring stress-strain descriptions via statistical averages weighted by a tensorial kernel. Nonlocal models range from strong nonlocality (strain type and displacement type, peridynamics) to weakly nonlocality (strain-gradient, stress-gradient, and higher-order models).

Definition 8.2.

RVE (a) is structurally entirely typical of the whole mixture in
R^{d}
on statistical average sense, and
(b) contains a sufficient number of inclusions for the apparent effective nonlocal operator (and statistically averaged field distributions in the phases) to be effectively independent of the applied inhomogeneous field.

Definition 8.2 omits the notion of a “large enough sample” and instead considers a heterogeneous medium (statistically homogeneous or periodic) in full space
R^{d}
. Rather than estimating effective moduli
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{*}
, it focuses on effective nonlocal operators of a given structure.

If
\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\rangle({\bf y})
varies slowly near
{\bf y}
, it allows for a Taylor expansion of its statistical average and the use of Fourier transforms. The most common effective nonlocal operator is a second-order differential operator

\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{*}\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})+\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{*}\nabla\otimes\nabla\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}).

(8.2)

The quasi-crystalline approximation by Lax
[
288
]
(
4.2
) is commonly used to truncate integral equation hierarchies, enabling explicit derivations of the nonlocal differential operator via methods like the effective field method
[
250
]
or conditional moments
[
255
]
. A key advantage of the rigorous approach
[
139
]
,
[
140
]
,
[
141
]
is its foundation in variational principles, providing approximation bounds
[
141
]
.
By combining MEFM with the Fourier transform method
[
141
]
, explicit expressions for the nonlocal overall operator as a second-order differential operator (
8.2
) were derived

[
52
]
,
[
103
]
.
For MEFM, the RVE size is
B^{\rm RVE{\bf L}*}=6a
(see Subsection 8.1), while for the corresponding nonlocal operator, it is

B^{\rm RVE{\cal L}*}=18a

[
52
]
,
[
53
]
. This approach, initially developed within the first background of micromechanics in LM
[
54
]
, was later extended to the second background in
[
81
]
.

We analyze periodic structure CMs in LM using CMic methods. For a fixed periodic body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
using variational and asymptotic expansion techniques (see Block 1 in (
3.2
)),
the approach in
[
426
]
constructs elliptic higher-order homogenized equations rigorously
(for the particular case of translation averaging solution (
5.6
)). However,
[
426
]
did not address numerical examples or RVE definitions. A quantitative assessment was performed in
[
14
]
on a two-phase composite under periodic anti-plane shear. Another method, second-order computational homogenization
[
264
]
,
[
265
]
, incorporates macroscopic deformation gradients and higher-order stress measures, resulting in a full second gradient continuum.
Analytical second-order homogenization of linear elastic CMs leads to the second gradient Mindlin continuum (see
[
80
]
), where the macroscopic length scale parameter depends on UC size. This approach highlights the impact of physical and geometrical nonlinearities on the relation between RVE size and macroscopic response.

The approach in
[
52
]
,
[
103
]
was later extended to PM in
[
97
]
. Equation (
3.24
) was analyzed under Hypothesis
H1a
(
3.8
) for linear operators
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal J$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i,j}
, which decompose into tensors when applied to constant effective fields (
3.8
).
The most general method, an iterative scheme (see LM counterpart in
[
81
]
), derives a nonlocal integral operator without assuming a predefined form. However, a major drawback is that solving the micromechanical problem (
3.24
) must be repeated for each different external loading
\langle\mbox{\boldmath$\vartheta$}\rangle({\bf z})
.
If the field
\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}_{j}\mbox{$\rangle$}
is smooth, it can be approximated using a Taylor expansion (see
[
166
]
), reducing the integral operator to a differential one (
8.2
). This method
[
97
]
can be adapted for periodic
structure CMs
[
98
]
by replacing the conditional probability density
\varphi(v_{q},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{k}|v_{i},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i})
by its
\delta
function form (
2.36
) in Eq. (
3.24
).

In Subsection 8.2, we identified two main shortcomings in the discussed methods.
The first issue is that the approaches for both random and periodic structure CMs (see
[
250
]
,
[
255
]

[
139
]
,
[
140
]
,
[
141
]
,

[
52
]
,
[
103
]
,
[
52
]
,
[
103
]
,

[
426
]
,
[
14
]
,
[
264
]
,
[
265
]
) are designed specifically for estimating the second-order gradient model effective operator (
8.2
). Their applicability to constructing a more general (e.g., integral) effective operator remains uncertain.
The second issue, related to
[
53
]
,
[
97
]
, is that these methods rely on the EFH (
3.8
) and assume linear phase properties. Additionally, when evaluating the effective response
\langle
\sigma
\rangle
under changing overall strains
\langle
\varepsilon
\rangle
, the entire micromechanical problem must be solved again from scratch.
In the next subsection, we introduce a universal method for estimating any form of the effective (surrogate) nonlocal operator for both random and periodic structure CMs. Its computational complexity matches that of the original approach in Subsection 86.1. Thus, in terms of generality and applicability (see Subsection 8.3 and Section 7), the methods in Subsection 8.2 are a dead end.

8.3
RVE for CMs subjected to body force with compact support

Not all datasets
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm p}
are suitable for ML and NN-based surrogate operators. A generalized RVE concept, extending Hill’s
[
217
]
definition, serves as a threshold to filter out inappropriate sub-datasets, ensuring compatibility with PM under self-equilibrated forcing
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
2.4
).

Definition 8.3.

RVE is structurally entirely typical of the whole CM area which is sufficient for all apparent effective parameters
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{I}
(
I={\rm r,p}
to be effectively stabilized outside RVE (as, e.g. in Eq. (
4.12
)) in the infinite random or
periodic structure CMs
with any elastic constitutive laws of phases.

We analyze the region
\overline{\rm RVE}=R^{d}\setminus{\rm RVE}
, where the dataset

\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
(or
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm p}
) stabilizes. Ensuring stabilization and selecting the correct
\overline{\rm RVE}=R^{d}\setminus{\rm RVE}
guarantees that all effective parameters between
|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|=B^{\rm RVE}
and
|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|=B^{\rm RVE}+B^{b}/2
remain consistent within a given tolerance. When this holds, the region
|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|>B^{\rm RVE}+B^{b}/2
can be disregarded, allowing an infinite medium to be approximated by a finite sample. A properly chosen RVE eliminates edge effects, typically confined to a boundary layer of thickness
6|\Omega|
if
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{\Omega}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(though this is not the case here; refer to p. 129 in
[
54
]
). Conversely, an undersized
B^{\rm RVE}
leads to numerical errors due to sample size limitations and edge effects (see Subsection 8.1).
In this framework, datasets
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm p}
are computed via micromechanical methods like CAM. Under the scale separation hypothesis (
2.33
2
) in LM, Definition 8.3 reduces to Hill’s classical definition
[
217
]
for apparent effective moduli
{}^{L}\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{*}
.

The RVE concept is even more critical in PM, where three types of nonlocal effects emerge: inhomogeneous applied fields
B^{b}
, Eq. (
2.4
), material nonlocality
l_{\delta}
, and inclusion interactions
a^{int}
, all of which interact synergistically. In contrast, in LM
[
217
]
, under the scale separation hypothesis (
2.33
2
), the nonlocal effects of
a^{int}
and
a
reduce to a constant.
For a self-equilibrated forcing term (
2.4
), statistical displacements
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
in a random heterogeneous bar (
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in R^{1}
) were analyzed in
[
83
]
using the AMic framework (see Fig. 1) . Results showed that for
|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|\geq a^{\rm l-r}
,
the solution
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\approx
const.; i.e. the domain

|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}|\leq a^{\rm l-r}
is RVE
{}^{\mbox{$\langle$}\bf u\mbox{$\rangle$}(\bf x)}
for
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
, depending on scale ratios
a/B^{b}/l_{\delta}
. These studies, though not explicitly naming RVE, pioneered its estimation in PM for random composites.
Additionally, RVEs must be assessed for other effective parameters, such as
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
(
4.2
) and
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm p}
(
5
), across different scale ratios
a/B^{b}/l^{(1)}_{\delta}/l^{(0)}_{\delta}
. Unlike LM (
8.1
), where
\rm RVE^{\bf L^{*}}\equiv RVE^{\mbox{$\langle$}\bf\epsilon\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}({\bf x})}
., these RVEs may differ for each scale ratio.

In peridynamic CMic, the term RVE is commonly used in both Block 3 (finite inclusion samples) and Block 2 (periodic structure CMs), as referenced in (
3.2
). The general limitations of Block 3 were discussed in Subsection 8.1.
For infinite periodic media, RVE has primarily been used in Block 2 as a shorthand for “unit cell (UC) of CM under remote homogeneous loading (
2.28
)” at extreme scale separations
\Lambda/L=\infty
,
\Lambda/|\Omega_{00}|=\infty
, and
L/|\Omega_{00}|=\infty
”, as seen in studies like

[
325
]
,
[
326
]
,
[
135
]
,
[
136
]
,

[
183
]
,
[
184
]
,

[
185
]
,
[
186
]
,
[
228
]
,

[
306
]
,
[
380
]
,
[
470
]
,
[
469
]
,
[
471
]
,
[
472
]
, and
[
454
]
.
While this shortcut is valid in LM under the scale separation hypothesis (
2.33
2
)
and has been widely accepted for decades, its direct application in PM requires caution.
In particular, using periodic boundary conditions (PBC, (
2.39
))—which are correct in LM—can be problematic in PM (see Subsection 2.3 and Comment 5.1). Instead, the variable periodic boundary conditions (VPBC) originally proposed in
[
83
]
should be further generalized to accommodate arbitrary scale ratios
|\Omega_{00}|/a/l^{(1)}_{\delta}/l^{(0)}_{\delta}
and unit cell geometries (see also Subsection 2.4).

This implies that the RVE for the
k
-th
implementation serves as a crucial parameter (or prerequisite) for building the dataset required for the surrogate operator (refer to Subsection 8.2). Specifically, if RVE
\subset w
, then
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}_{k}

is incorporated into
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
. Otherwise, these elements
are excluded from further analysis of the surrogate operator.
The dimensions
B^{\rm RVE}_{k}
of the RVE may vary across different implementations indexed by
k
-th. The primary role of the RVE is to ensure the stabilization of the field variables within the domain
\overline{\rm RVE}_{k}
, rather than merely serving as a mechanism to determine the size of a subdomain for modeling the medium. In this context, the RVE is not strictly a tool for selecting a characteristic size of subdomain (see, e.g.,
[
217
]
) of the micro inhomogeneous medium but is instead a means of achieving convergence in the statistical or spatial distribution of field quantities.
This does not imply that the final size of the RVE,
B^{\rm RVE}_{\rm final}
, is equal to
\max_{k}B^{\rm RVE}_{k}
. Once the contribution
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}_{k}
has been accepted into the overall dataset
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
, the specific value of
B^{\rm RVE}_{k}
associated with that particular
k
-the implementation may no longer be retained or considered.
Furthermore, it is well-established in LM that increasing the sample size effectively mitigates the sample size effect (see, e.g.,
[
252
]
,
[
440
]
). However, even resolving the sample size effect does not address boundary layer and edge effects associated with nonlocal phenomena. The primary advantage of the new RVE concept (as defined) lies in its ability to completely eliminate boundary layer and edge effects for random and periodic structured composite materials, irrespective of the phases’ elastic properties—whether local or nonlocal, linear or nonlinear.

For estimating any effective nonlocal operator, including surrogate models in ML or NN
(see Section 9), analyzing field distributions and determining the appropriate RVE size for a finite scale ratio
B^{b}/|\Omega_{00}|\not=\infty
is crucial. To the author’s knowledge, the term RVE has not been explicitly used in LM or PM concerning the forcing term (
2.4
).
The RVE depends on scale ratios
a/B^{\rm RVE}/l^{(1)}_{\delta}/l^{(0)}_{\delta}
and varies with the gradient
\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
. This means two functions
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}^{\prime}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
with identical scale ratios may yield different RVEs. Additionally, satisfying
b({\bf 0},B^{b})\subset\Omega_{00}
does not guarantee RVE
\subset\Omega_{00}
;
in fact, the RVE may span multiple unit cells.
Despite this complexity, the RVE remains a key benchmark: if the solution domain
w
of Eqs.
(4.10)-(4.20) fully contains the RVE (RVE
\subset w
), the computed elements
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm I}_{k}
(
I={\rm r,p},\ k=1,\ldots,N
)
contribute to the surrogate operator dataset (Subsection 8.2).
Otherwise,
w
must be expanded or
|\nabla\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})|
reduced.
If the RVE size RVE
\subset w
, then
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm I}_{k}
varies across implementations, ensuring field stabilization rather than merely defining a subdomain for modeling.
Unlike traditional LM approaches, where increasing the sample size mitigates finite-size effects
[
252
]
,
[
440
]
, this novel RVE concept eliminates boundary layer and edge effects in random or periodic composites, regardless of phase properties—local or nonlocal, linear or nonlinear.

Comment 8.1.
Special attention is given to statistically inhomogeneous (or functionally graded, FG) composites, where the arrangement of elements (such as concentration and orientation, see
[
80
]
) depends on position, as described in Eq. (
2.35
). FG composites were studied using the multiparticle effective field method (MEFM) within the framework of the EFH (
3.1
), where the exact GIE (3.14) is simplified to Eq. (3.13). The area where Eq. (
3.14
) should be solved is defined as
w^{\rm FG}=B^{\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}}\oplus b(0,a^{\rm int})
; here,
B^{\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}}
represents a domain where
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\neq\text{const}
, and
a^{\rm int}
is the scale of long-range interactions between inclusions (
2.33
). However, a new rigorous Definition 8.3 is immediately beneficial for FG composites with random structures (
2.35
).
In statistically homogeneous media, the RVE is invariant to parallel translation, meaning that if
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\to\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}
(
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}+\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}
), then RVE(
\bf x
) = RVE(
\bf y
) for any
\bf z
. However, in statistically inhomogeneous (functionally graded) media (
2.35
), this invariance is lost, so RVE(
\bf x
)
\not=
RVE(
\bf y
). As a result, the AGIE (3.23) should be solved for a set of uncoupled solutions of RVE(
\bf x
) instead of a less effective coupled solution over the domain
w^{\rm FG}
. The resulting datasets
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
, depending on body force
\bf b
, are not invariant (in contrast to
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
(
4.2
)) under parallel translation along the cross-section of FG composites.

8.4
RVE for deterministic structure CMs

Random or periodic structure CMs are characterized by general probability densities (
\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})
and
\varphi(v_{i},{\bf x}_{i}|;v_{j},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{j})
, see Subsection 2.3) or specific cases (
2.36
) with a periodic grid
\Lambda
. In contrast, deterministic structure CMs do not exhibit randomness or periodicity. For example,
from microcomputer tomography (micro-CT) or scanning electron microscopy (SEM)
images of composite samples and typically consist of
several hundreds or thousands of inclusions
[
153
]
.
Notably, CM images obtained through CT techniques can be viewed as observational snapshots (“window of observation”) of deterministic structure CMs. The observation window
w
with boundary conditions (either (
2.28
) or (
2.29
)) is shown
in Fig. 2.

Fig. 2:
CT image with BC

As discussed in Subsection 8.2, even with a sufficiently large window
w
for estimating
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf L$}^{*}
and the application of highly precise numerical methods in DNS, the boundary layer effect persists and cannot be fully eliminated. The difficulties in using finite-size samples to approximate effective behavior are well recognized in LM (see, e.g.,
[
80
]
, p. 593;
[
54
]
, pp. 226-229).
It is known that determining the overall behavior of a sample
w
using DNS, such as, e.g., in Eq. (4.22),

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm DNS}=\{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm DNS}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N},\ \ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm DNS}_{k}=\{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\},\ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in w,

(8.3)

results in the complete loss of information related to boundary layer effects;
Here, the applied body force

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
does not necessarily conform to Eq. (
2.4
).
For example, Fig. 3a (Fig. 3 is reproduced from in
[
491
]
) presents an experimental counterpart of Fig. 2, showing a biological tissue specimen under biaxial stretching, with the digital image correlation (DIC) displacement tracking grid marked in green. A schematic of a specimen subjected to Dirichlet-type boundary conditions is illustrated in Fig. 3b. Additionally, a neural network-based learning model was utilized to develop a surrogate operator capable of predicting overall displacement fields for previously unseen loading conditions in finite samples of soft biological tissue (see Fig. 3c).
Figures 3a-3c from
[

have been reproduced in several key studies on physics-informed neural operators (e.g.,

[

Fig. 3:
Biological tissue specimen

A refined version of Maxwell’s approach
[
339
]
, often referred to as its “second birth,” represents an infinite statistically homogeneous (or periodic) CM as an inclusion cloud embedded within an infinite matrix
[
405
]
. Unlike the classical Maxwell model
[
339
]
, these generalized approaches account for the interactions between inclusions within the cloud.
However, Buryachenko
[
82
]
demonstrated that 23 key papers in this field
[
405
]
are fundamentally flawed when using homogeneous remote BC (
2.28
). To address this, BC (
2.28
) can be replaced with freeloading at infinity, supplemented by a body force (
2.4
). This allows the inclusion cloud (of learnable size) to estimate the RVE per Definition 8.3. Through Monte Carlo simulations of “random” inclusion sets, we can determine
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm p}
similarly to (
5.6
), replacing uniform
\chi
in (
5.6
) with random inclusion sets. At this stage, the microstructure of the cloud and its computation method (e.g., multipole expansion
[
405
]
) become irrelevant. The final step involves using ML or NN techniques (see Section 9) to derive a surrogate nonlocal operator for modeling infinite statistically homogeneous media. This marks the “third birth” of Maxwell’s approach, proving highly effective.

The concept of the “inclusion cloud” in Maxwell’s approach
[
339
]
can be reformulated by replacing the remote BC
(
2.28
) (or (
2.29
)),
with a body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
2.4
) that has compact support.
Under this modification, the domain of interest
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in w
(
8.3
), effectively reduces to the
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in
RVE in the sense of Definition 8.3. The DNS data can then be represented as

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm DNSd}=\{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm DNSd}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N},\ \ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm DNSd}_{k}=\{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\},\ \ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in{\rm RVE}.

(8.4)

The loading configuration of a deterministic structure within the domain
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in w
(as depicted in Fig. 2) by a body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
2.4
) is illustrated in Fig. 4. In this figure, the localized force application region
b(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}_{i},B^{b})\subset{\rm RVE}
is explicitly shown.
This highlights that the applied force is confined within a subset of the RVE, ensuring the stabilization of effective parameters
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm DNSd}
outside RVE. In this approach, the dataset
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm DNSd}
is constructed from various realizations of the body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
and the microstructural configurations (e.g., derived from distinct CT images). Generations of deterministic structures at the fixed
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
allow the analog of dataset (
5
).
The generation of deterministic structures while keeping the body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
fixed enables the construction of an analogous dataset to Eq. (
5
)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm d}

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm d}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N},

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm d}_{k}

\displaystyle\!=\!

\displaystyle\{\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\!\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\!\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\!\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}^{(1)}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\},

(8.5)

where the statistical averages
\mbox{$\langle$}(\cdot)\mbox{$\rangle$}
are computed by averaging over the ensemble of configurations, ensuring a statistical representation of the mechanical response for the given loading conditions.

Fig. 4:
Scheme of CT image with
b({\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}}_{i},B^{b})\subset{\rm RVE}\subset w

In the context of computational micromechanics for 2D particular realizations of statistically homogeneous random structures (i.e., deterministic structures), Silling
et al.

[
422
]
proposed a coarse-graining model for the upscaling of mechanical properties of composite materials (CMs). This model is based on Monte Carlo-simulated inclusion placements (
\approx
900 inclusions) within a finite-sized square domain
w
, which is subjected to a self-equilibrated body force with compact support
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(
2.4
).
The body force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
corresponds to the region of long-range interaction, as described in
[
83
]
. The geometric scale separation is characterized by the ratio

{\rm dist}(\partial{\rm RVE},\partial w)\approx 10a=100\lambda
,
where represents the lattice spacing.
This scale separation satisfies the conditions of an RVE according to Definition 8.3.
The validity of this RVE assumption is supported by the numerical observation that the effective strain field
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

vanishes in the vicinity of
\partial w
, as illustrated in colored Fig. 12 of
[
422
]
, which is consistent
with Fig. 4. Notably, the term “RVE” was not explicitly used in
[
422
]
.
The purple regions, where
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})\approx{\bf 0}
, and the blue regions in Fig. 5 correspond to the areas outside
w\setminus
RVE and inside RVE, respectively, as shown in Fig. 4.
The proposed upscaled peridynamic model allows for a significantly coarser discretization compared to the original fine-scale model, which relies on DNS. This coarse-graining approach enables large-scale simulations to be conducted efficiently, through the estimation of quantities such as

\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\ \mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
,
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}\in v_{i},\ \mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in R^{d}

remains outside the scope of the study.

Strain
\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\varepsilon$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
in a sample
w

Amic functions like a solar system, with GIE (

The scheme of the CAM (
3.17
), (
3.19
), (
4.5
)-(
4.7
) is represented in a block diagram in Fig. 6. To simplify notation, the scheme corresponds to the iterative solution of Eq. (
4.5
)-(
4.7
).
At its core, the process revolves around Block 3

Fig. 6:
The iteration scheme of the CAM (
4.5
)

Perturbator and Block 4 Micromechanics, which form a central feedback loop. Block 3 computes the perturbator
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}})
(
3.17
). The preprocessing Block 1 Input provides Block 3 with essential data, including the microstructural geometry of an inclusion
v_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
within an infinite homogeneous matrix (or a large sample), material properties
\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
(such as micromodulus
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf C$}^{\rm bond}
), and a square mesh
\Omega^{\rm sq}:=\{(x_{1},...,x_{d})^{\top}|x_{i}=hp_{i}\}

(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf p$}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{d})\in Z^{d})
.
Similarly, Block 2 Input supplies Block 4 with geometrical information, specifically the same square mesh
\Omega^{\rm sq}

and the spatial distribution of inclusions
X
. For random structures, this distribution is characterized by probability densities
\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j})
and conditional densities
\varphi(v_{j},{\bf x}_{j}|;v_{i},{\bf x}_{i})
. For the periodic structures
X=\mbox{\boldmath$\Lambda$}
whereas
X
is a deterministic field for deterministic structure CMs.
The use of the same mesh
\Omega^{\rm sq}
in Block 3 and Block 4 is primarily for ease of integration between these two components (see the case of LM on p. 428 in
[
80
]
). In the iterative cycle [Block 3]
\rightleftharpoons
[Block 4] (
4.5
), Block 3 takes as input
\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

and outputs
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}^{\theta\zeta}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{$\langle$}\overline{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i})
, where
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$}\in\Omega^{\rm sq}
.
However, the mesh
\Omega^{\rm sq}
is used solely to maintain consistency between Block 3 and Block 4 and does not determine the internal solution method within Block 3 (see Eqs. (
3.17
)). Importantly, the internal workings of Block 3 are irrelevant to Block 4, and vice versa. As a result, these blocks function as independent “Black Boxes” that interact solely through their inputs and outputs, as indicated in Fig. 6 by their black shading. Output Block 5 contains the datasets either
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
,
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm p}
, or
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm d}
for CMs with either random, periodic, or deterministic structures, respectively.
It should be mentioned that particular cases of problems indicated in Block 3 in Eq. (
3.2
) can be considered as a Block 3 Perturbator (see Fig. 6) for one inclusion (or crack) in a big sample.

The AGIE (
3.23
) plays a fundamental role in both Amic and Cmic within the PM framework. Sections 3 and 4 focus on the formulation of AGIE (
3.23
) and its solution (
4.1
)–(
4.20
), establishing it as the core component of the self-consistent method Gr4) (
4.1
)–(
4.20
) in Amic (see Fig. 7). The perturbation methods Gr2) can be derived from Gr4) by taking the limit
c\to 0
.
Furthermore, the model methods Gr1) corresponding to (
3.2
)
(referenced in the first paragraph of this subsection) implicitly or explicitly rely on EFH (
3.8
), which is eliminated under body force loading (
2.4
). Consequently, the physical interpretation of Gr1) methods under such loading conditions remains unclear.
Variational methods, widely used in LM (see
[
80
]
for references), have been extended to PM in
[
80
]
,
[
79
]
for statistically homogeneous materials and field parameters. However, even for statistically homogeneous media subjected to body force loading (
2.4
), the statistical homogeneity of the field is disrupted, making the formulation of variational methods for this loading nontrivial.
In the Cmic branch, for periodic structured CMs, the equations (
5.1
)–(
5.3
) derived from CAM (
3.25
) formally resemble the equations (
4.11
)–(
4.13
) for random structured CMs. Additionally, the translation averaging (
5.6
) applied to periodic structures leads to equations (
5.7
)–(
5.11
), which are structurally similar to (
4.15
)–(
4.19
) for random structures. This process generates the dataset
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm p}
(
5
), which is analogous to
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
(
4.2
).
Notably, periodic boundary conditions (
2.39
), (
2.40
) (or VPBC (
2.39
), (
2.40
)) are effectively eliminated, raising questions about the generalization of methods from Blocks 1 and 2 (see (
3.2
) and the first paragraph of this Subsection) for body force loading (
2.4
) in both LM and PM. Ultimately, CAM reduces the analysis of periodic structured CMs to the study of a finite deterministic field of inclusions, aligning it with the methods of Block 3 (see Fig. 7). This suggests that DNSs within Cmic are systematically reorganized within Amic to construct datasets
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm p}
(
5
) and
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm d}
(
8.4
), both structurally analogous to
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
(
4.2
).

Fig. 7:
AGIE as a central governing equation of PM

The fundamental framework and key results of this paper—embodied in Eqs. (
3.17
)-(
3.26
), (
4.7
)-(
4.2
), (
5.1
)-(
5
), (
6.2
)-(
6.12
), and Subsections 8.3 and 8.4—are formulated in an operator form that remains entirely independent of any specific constitutive law of elasticity. This universality ensures that these equations hold for any constitutive model, including, e.g., those detailed in Section 7.
In essence, the representation of PD using Eqs. (
2.1
)-(
2.27
) and (
3.1
)-(
3.1
) can be seamlessly substituted with the more detailed constitutive formulations presented in Section 7, without affecting the overall structure and validity of the results, see Sections 3-8. The choice of PD as the primary modeling approach in this work does not stem from an inherent superiority over other models but rather from the author’s long-standing dedication to peridynamic CMs—an expertise and intellectual commitment developed over more than a decade of focused research in this field.

9
Estimation of a set of surrogate operators

Silling’s pioneering work
[
418
]
,
[
487
]
, later expanded in
[
489
]
, introduced data-driven ML techniques for modeling composite materials (CMs) by constructing surrogate nonlocal operators from DNS. These studies focused on a finite 1D heterogeneous bar under wave loading at the boundary and oscillatory forcing.

In the current (see for details
[
83
]
,
[
84
]
), Silling’s approach
[
418
]
is adapted by replacing the DNS dataset
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm DNS}
(
8.3
) with
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm r}
(
4.2
),
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm p}
(
5
) or
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}^{\rm d}
(
8.4
), corresponding to random, periodic, and deterministic structures, respectively. The inhomogeneous forcing term
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
with compact support (
2.4
) serves both as a loading parameter and a tool for learning surrogate nonlocal constitutive laws for CMs.
The surrogate datasets
\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm r}
,
\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm p}
,
\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm d}
approximate their respective datasets while efficiently compressing micromechanical data. The field PM dataset
\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm r}
is constructed via a surrogate model
{\cal S}
, mapping statistical micromechanical averages to macroscopic fields:

\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm r}={\cal S}({\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm r})
.
This approach retains essential micromechanical features while simplifying data representation.

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{\rm\gamma}[\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}](\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bf\gamma}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{\rm\gamma}[\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}](\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\mbox{\boldmath$\bf K$}_{\gamma}(|\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$}|)(\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$})-\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}}_{k}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}))~d\mbox{\boldmath$\bf y$},

(9.1)

where
\gamma:=b,{\rm\sigma},{\rm u_{i}},{\rm\sigma}_{i}
and
{\mbox{\boldmath$\Gamma$}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}):=-\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\mbox{$\rangle$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}),\mbox{$\langle$}\mbox{\boldmath$\sigma$}\mbox{$\rangle$}_{i}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf z$},\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
, respectively, correspond to four surrogate operators
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{\gamma}
.
Each of these fields corresponds to a surrogate operator
\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{\gamma}
associated with the problem.
The objective is to construct an optimal surrogate model for the kernel functions

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf K$}_{\gamma}^{*}
, which define the surrogate datasett
\widetilde{\mbox{\boldmath$\cal D$}}^{\rm r}
.
This is formulated as four optimization problems, one for each
\gamma

\displaystyle\!\!\!\mbox{\boldmath$\bf K$}_{\gamma}^{*}={\rm arg}\!\min_{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{{\bf K}_{\gamma}}}\!\sum_{k=1}^{N}\!||\mbox{\boldmath$\cal L$}_{\rm{\gamma}}[\mbox{$\langle$}{\mbox{\boldmath$\bf u$}_{k}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k})\mbox{$\rangle$}](\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})-{\bf\Gamma}_{k}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})||^{2}_{l_{2}}+{\cal R}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf K$}_{\gamma}).

(9.2)

The objective function quantifies the discrepancy between the surrogate model and the original dataset using an
l_{2}
-norm over
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in R^{d}
, with a regularization term
{\cal R}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf K$}_{\gamma})
(e.g., Tikhonov regularization) to improve conditioning.
To optimize
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf K$}_{\gamma}
, the Adam optimizer
[
261
]
is employed in an iterative gradient descent scheme, where
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf K$}_{\gamma}
is expressed as a linear combination of Bernstein-based polynomials. Further details are available in
[
487
]
, with additional insights in
[
167
]
,
[
490
]
,
[
492
]
, and
[
489
]
.

The approaches in
[
487
]
and
[
489
]
use uncompressed datasets (
8.3
), unlike the compressed methodology in (
4.2
) (or (
5
)). The DNS dataset
{\cal D}^{\rm DNS}
(
8.3
) captures detailed microscale displacements

\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$}\in w
for each applied force
\mbox{\boldmath$\bf b$}_{k}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bf x$})
, making it significantly larger than

{\cal D}^{\rm I}
(
I={\rm r,p,d}
) (
4.2
) even for identical loadings.
Compression in
{\cal D}^{\rm I}
does not require full-field DNS computations but instead leverages micromechanics to estimate effective parameters more efficiently. On the other side, for homogeneous linearized peridynamic medium (
2.12
) and
(
2.13
) subjected to remote homogeneous BC (
2.28
) (e.g.
\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}(\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\xi$}
), the corresponding local moduli can be obtained from the equation of constitutive law (see
[
425
]
).
Seemingly, for surrogate homogeneous media subjected to remote homogeneous BC (
2.28
), we obtain
